Hodge properties of Airy moments Thanks: This research was partly supported by the PICS project TWN 8094 from CNRS
Claude Sabbah, Jeng-Daw Yu

Abstract
. We consider the complex analogues of symmetric power moments of cubic exponential sums. These are symmetric powers of the classical Airy differential equation. We show that their de Rham cohomologies underlie an arithmetic Hodge structure in the sense of Anderson and we compute their Hodge numbers by means of the irregular Hodge filtration, which is indexed by rational numbers, on their realizations as exponential mixed Hodge structures. The main result is that all Hodge numbers are either zero or one.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
14C30, 14D07, 32G20, 32S40, 34M35

1.
Introduction

Families of cubic exponential sums attached to the polynomial
x^{3}+zx
in
x
over finite fields and their symmetric power moments over
z

(Airy moments) have been considered in analogy with moments of Kloosterman sums for example, and much is already known about the corresponding
L
functions (see
[
11
,
12
]
and the references therein). For the analogous moments of Kloosterman sums, a conjecture of Broadhurst and Roberts, concerning the precise functional equations satisfied
by the corresponding global
L
functions, has been proved in
[
7
]
(except the exact local information at prime
2
when
k
is even).
In such a case, a pure Nori motive
\Motive_{k}
defined over
\mathbf{Q}
is attached to the
k
-moments.
One of the main arguments is to prove that
the associated Galois representation of
\Motive_{k}

is potentially automorphic, which relies on a theorem of Patrikis and Taylor
[
19
]
, owing to the property proved in
[
7
]
, that all nonzero Hodge numbers of
\Motive_{k}
are equal to one.

For Airy moments, a strictly similar strategy to understand the Galois theoretic properties of the corresponding motivic object
\Motive_{k}
is not possible, basically because
\Motive_{k}
is not a Nori motive,
but an
ulterior motive
in the sense of Anderson
[
3
]
. Nevertheless, Hodge theory can be developed in this context (arithmetic Hodge structure in the sense of Anderson), with the caveat that the Hodge numbers
h^{\mathsf{p},\mathsf{q}}
may occur for rational exponents
\mathsf{p},\mathsf{q}
subject to
\mathsf{p}+\mathsf{q}\in\mathbf{Z}
.

A similar notion of Hodge structure, that we call a
finite monodromic mixed Hodge structure
as defined in Section
2
,
has been considered by Scherk and Steenbrink
[
34
]
when analyzing the join of two mixed Hodge structures equipped with an automorphism of finite order. Already in
[
36
]
, Steenbrink attached to the mixed Hodge structure on the nearby cycles and vanishing cycles of a holomorphic function with an isolated singularity, a Hodge polynomial in
\mathbf{Z}[t^{1/m},t^{-1/m}]
, where
m
is the order of the semi-simple part of the monodromy.
He conjectured a behaviour of the Hodge polynomial
under Thom-Sebastiani sums as modeled in
[
34
]
, a conjecture which was proved by M. Saito
[
32
]
and later received a proof using motivic integration in
[
10
]
.

The complex analogue of the Airy
k
-moment is the
k
-th symmetric product
\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai}
of the Airy differential equation
\mathrm{Ai}
on the affine line
\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{z}
, defined by the classical Airy operator
\partial_{z}^{2}-z
. We regard
\mathrm{Ai}
as a rank-two vector bundle on
\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{z}
with a connection having
\infty
as its only singularity, which is irregular of slope
3/2
and has irregularity number equal to
3
. Therefore,
\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai}
is regarded as a rank
(k+1)
vector bundle with connection on
\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{z}
having a singularity at infinity only. Contrary to the case of the Kloosterman connection, the de Rham cohomology
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
does not naturally underlie a mixed Hodge structure (again reflecting the fact that
\Motive_{k}
is not classical).
However, it is the de Rham fiber of an exponential mixed Hodge structure with finite monodromy, hence underlies a finite monodromic mixed Hodge structure
(see Section
5.c
).
As indicated above, such structures provide an alternative approach to the arithmetic Hodge structures of Anderson, and they are more closely related to exponential mixed Hodge structures defined by Kontsevich and Soibelman
[
15
]
. As in
[
7
]
, we recover the
\mathbf{Q}
-indexed Hodge filtration of this finite monodromic mixed Hodge structure as the irregular Hodge filtration on the de Rham fiber
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
. The main result of this article is the computation of the corresponding irregular Hodge numbers, opening the way to applying the results of Patrikis and Taylor on the arithmetic of the underlying ulterior motive
\Motive_{k}
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
k
be an integer
\geqslant 2
and set
k^{\prime}=\left\lfloor(k-1)/2\right\rfloor
.
Then the de Rham cohomology
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
naturally underlies
a finite monodromic mixed Hodge structure and the Hodge numbers

h^{\mathsf{p}\mathsf{q}}=\dim\gr_{F}^{\mathsf{p}}\gr^{W}_{\mathsf{p}+\mathsf{q}}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})

are all equal to one for the following
(\mathsf{p},\mathsf{q})

and vanish otherwise:

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

k
odd,

\mathsf{p}+\mathsf{q}=k+1

and
\mathsf{p}=\frac{1}{3}(k+2i)
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant k^{\prime}+1
,

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

4\mid(k+2)
,
\mathsf{p}+\mathsf{q}=k+1

and
\min\{\mathsf{p},\mathsf{q}\}=\frac{1}{3}(k+2i)
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant{k^{\prime}}/{2}
,

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

4\mid k

and either
\mathsf{p}+\mathsf{q}=k+1

with
\min\{\mathsf{p},\mathsf{q}\}=\frac{1}{3}(k+2i)
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant{(k^{\prime}-1)}/{2}
,
or
\mathsf{p}=\mathsf{q}={(k+2)}/{2}
.

We are mostly interested in the middle de Rham cohomology, which is by definition the image
\image[\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})]
. We show (Corollaries
5.14
and
5.22
) that, with respect to the finite monodromic mixed Hodge structure on
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
, it is equal to the weight
(k+1)
subspace:

\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})=W_{k+1}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai}).

As a consequence of Theorem
1.1
, the middle de Rham cohomology underlies a finite monodromic pure Hodge structure, and the corresponding Hodge numbers coincide with those of
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
if
4\nmid k
, and are obtained by omitting the case
\mathsf{p}=\mathsf{q}={(k+2)}/{2}
if
4\mid k
.

Organization of the paper

The approach of the present article is much inspired from that of
[
7
]
on moments of Kloosterman connections. Sections
2
–
5
consist of a review of known properties of various tools that we use, together with complementary results in presence of an action of the group
{\widehat{\mu}}=\varprojlim_{m}\mathbf{Z}/m\mathbf{Z}
. In Section
5
,
we emphasize the decomposition of a finite monodromic exponential mixed Hodge structure
(that we call a
{\widehat{\mu}}
-exponential mixed Hodge structure for short)
into its classical and non-classical parts. While the former can be treated with the same methods as in
[
7
]
, the latter shows new Hodge-theoretic phenomena.

The main example of such an object is that attached to a regular function on
\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{s}\times V
of the form
s^{m}g
(
m\geqslant 2
), where
g:V\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{A}^{\!1}
is a regular function on a smooth quasi-projective variety. The corresponding non-classical
{\widehat{\mu}}
-exponential mixed Hodge structure (Proposition
5.10
) can be expressed by means of the mixed Hodge structure of the covering of
V\smallsetminus g^{-1}(0)
which is cyclic of degree
m
and ramified along
g^{-1}(0)
.
While this expression makes clear the ulterior motivic origin of this finite monodromic mixed Hodge structure, it does not help for computing Hodge numbers in Theorem
1.1
. In order to handle the case of even
k
in the theorem, we provide in Section
5.f
an expression in terms of additive convolution with Kummer sheaves, which reduces the computation to a local (monodromic) computation similar to that used for the classical component modeled on that of
[
7
]
.

In Section
6
we provide Hodge-theoretic properties for the moments of the generalized Airy equation of order
n\geqslant 2
(the case
n=2
being our main concern),
yielding the finite monodromic mixed Hodge structure
on the de Rham cohomologies of their symmetric powers
\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai}_{n}
, and an estimation of their weights. This is a variant of the techniques used in
[
7
]
.

Once all these preliminaries are settled, the proof of Theorem
1.1
is achieved in Section
7
. As in loc. cit., we play with two expressions of the de Rham cohomology, one computed in dimension one like
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
and the other one using the geometry of the function
s^{3}\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\frac{1}{3}x_{i}^{3}-x_{i})
. The former provides in a simple way a basis of the de Rham cohomology and an associated filtration which satisfies the conclusion of the theorem, and the latter relates the computation of Hodge numbers to that of the corresponding
irregular Hodge filtration
on a suitable blow-up of
\mathbb{P}^{k+1}
. While the geometry is simple enough to enable us to conclude the proof if
k
is odd by showing that the basis filtration is contained in the irregular Hodge filtration, we need supplementary arguments to conclude in the case of even
k
, which occupies Sections
7.d
–
7.g
. Although the idea is similar to that of
[
7
]
, we need here to make use of a Hodge-theoretic inverse stationary phase formula, as we already did in
[
29
]
, to recover enough information on the Hodge filtration.

2.
Finite monodromic mixed Hodge structures

We review and adapt results of
[
34
, p. 661]
. Let
{\widehat{\mu}}=\varprojlim_{m}\mathbf{Z}/m\mathbf{Z}

be the profinite completion of the abelian group
\mathbf{Z}
.
Let
\Vect(\mathbf{Q})
be the category of finite-dimensional
\mathbf{Q}
-vector spaces and let
\Vect^{\widehat{\mu}}(\mathbf{Q})
denote the category
consisting of a finite-dimensional
\mathbf{Q}
-vector space
H_{\mathbf{Q}}

together with an action of
{\widehat{\mu}}
through a finite quotient
\mathbf{Z}/m\mathbf{Z}
for some
m
.
Equivalently,
by setting
T
to be the action of the topological generator
1\in{\widehat{\mu}}
,
an object of
\Vect^{\widehat{\mu}}(\mathbf{Q})
is a pair
(H_{\mathbf{Q}},T)

consisting of the space
H_{\mathbf{Q}}
together with an automorphism
T
of finite order.
Each group ring
\mathbf{Q}[\mathbf{Z}/m\mathbf{Z}]
has the orthogonal decomposition

\mathbf{Q}[\mathbf{Z}/m\mathbf{Z}]=e_{1}\mathbf{Q}[\mathbf{Z}/m\mathbf{Z}]\oplus e_{\neq 1}\mathbf{Q}[\mathbf{Z}/m\mathbf{Z}]

where
e_{1}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{g\in\mathbf{Z}/m\mathbf{Z}}g

is the projector into the invariant part and
e_{\neq 1}=1-e_{1}
,
and the decomposition is compatible with the quotient

\mathbf{Z}/m^{\prime}\mathbf{Z}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{Z}/m\mathbf{Z}
for
m\mid m^{\prime}
.
Correspondingly for a given object
(H_{\mathbf{Q}},T)
of
\Vect^{\widehat{\mu}}(\mathbf{Q})
,
there is a canonical decomposition in
\Vect^{\widehat{\mu}}(\mathbf{Q})

(H_{\mathbf{Q}},T)=(H_{\mathbf{Q},1},\id)\oplus(H_{\mathbf{Q},\neq 1},T),

where
T
has no non-trivial invariant in
H_{\mathbf{Q},\neq 1}
.
Let
\mu_{m}\subset\mathbf{C}^{\times}
be the cyclic subgroup of order
m

and
\mu_{m}^{*}=\mu_{m}\smallsetminus\{1\}
.
In terms of the eigenvalue decomposition

H_{\mathbf{C}}=\mathop{\textstyle\bigoplus}\displaylimits_{\zeta\in\mu_{m}}H_{\zeta}

where
H_{\mathbf{C}}=H_{\mathbf{Q}}\otimes\mathbf{C}

and
T=\zeta\id
on
H_{\zeta}
,
one has

H_{\mathbf{Q},1}\otimes\mathbf{C}=H_{1}

and
H_{\mathbf{Q},\neq 1}\otimes\mathbf{C}=\mathop{\textstyle\bigoplus}\displaylimits_{\zeta\in\mu_{m}^{*}}H_{\zeta}
.
Notice that
\overline{H_{\zeta}}=H_{1/\zeta}

under complex conjugation. Finally the category
\Vect^{\widehat{\mu}}(\mathbf{Q})

is equipped with its natural tensor structure.

Let
\MHS
be the category of (graded polarizable) mixed Hodge structures
(H_{\mathbf{Q}},F^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}H_{\mathbf{C}},W_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}H_{\mathbf{Q}})
.
We define the category
\MHS({\widehat{\mu}})
of

finite monodromic mixed Hodge structures

as the category consisting of objects of
\MHS

equipped with an action of

{\widehat{\mu}}
through the quotient
\mathbf{Z}/m\mathbf{Z}
for some
m

(equivalently, with an automorphism
T
of finite order as above).
Morphisms are morphisms of mixed Hodge structures compatible with the group action. In order to express in a simple way the tensor product in this category,
we consider the category
\MHS^{\widehat{\mu}}
of
{\widehat{\mu}}
-mixed Hodge structures.
The objects of
\MHS^{\widehat{\mu}}
take the form
((H_{\mathbf{Q}},T),F^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}_{\widehat{\mu}}H_{\mathbf{C}},W_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}^{\widehat{\mu}}H_{\mathbf{Q}})
, where

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

(H_{\mathbf{Q}},T)
is an object of
\Vect^{\widehat{\mu}}(\mathbf{Q})
,
so that
H_{\mathbf{Q}}=H_{\mathbf{Q},1}\oplus H_{\mathbf{Q},\neq 1}

and
H_{\mathbf{C}}=\mathop{\textstyle\bigoplus}\displaylimits_{\zeta\in\mu_{m}}H_{\zeta}
;

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

F^{\mathsf{p}}_{\widehat{\mu}}H_{\mathbf{C}}=\mathop{\textstyle\bigoplus}\displaylimits_{\zeta}F^{\mathsf{p}}_{\widehat{\mu}}H_{\zeta}
is an exhaustive decreasing filtration indexed by
\mathsf{p}\in\mathbf{Q}
, and for each
\zeta=\exp(-2\pi\mathsf{i}a)
with
a\in(-1,0]\cap\mathbf{Q}
, the filtration
F^{\mathsf{p}}_{\widehat{\mu}}H_{\zeta}
jumps at most at
\mathbf{Z}-a
;

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

W_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}^{\widehat{\mu}}H_{\mathbf{Q}}=W_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}^{\widehat{\mu}}H_{1}\oplus W_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}^{\widehat{\mu}}H_{\neq 1}
is an exhaustive increasing filtration indexed by
\mathbf{Z}
,

satisfying the following property: setting

(2.1)

\begin{split}F^{p}H_{\mathbf{C}}&=\mathop{\textstyle\bigoplus}\displaylimits_{\zeta\in\mu_{m}}F^{p-a}_{\widehat{\mu}}H_{\zeta},\quad p\in\mathbf{Z},\\
W_{\ell}H_{\mathbf{Q}}&=W_{\ell}^{\widehat{\mu}}H_{1}\oplus W^{\widehat{\mu}}_{\ell+1}H_{\neq 1},\end{split}

we impose that
(H_{\mathbf{Q}},F^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}H_{\mathbf{C}},W_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}H_{\mathbf{Q}})
is a (graded polarizable) mixed Hodge structure. Morphisms are the natural ones. With the induced automorphism
T
,
the latter becomes an object of
\MHS({\widehat{\mu}})
.

Lemma 2.2
.

The natural functor
\MHS^{\widehat{\mu}}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\MHS({\widehat{\mu}})
defined by (
2.1
) is an equivalence of categories. Any morphism in
\MHS^{\widehat{\mu}}
is bi-strict with respect to
F^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}_{\widehat{\mu}},W_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}^{\widehat{\mu}}
.

Proof.

Let
((H_{\mathbf{Q}},F^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}H_{\mathbf{C}},W_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}H_{\mathbf{Q}}),T)

be an object of
\MHS({\widehat{\mu}})
.
We note that the Hodge filtration is compatible with the eigenvalue decomposition of
H_{\mathbf{C}}
with respect to
T
, being stable by
T
, and a similar property for the weight filtration, so that the correspondence given by

(2.3)

\displaystyle F_{\widehat{\mu}}^{\mathsf{p}}H_{\zeta}

\displaystyle=F^{\mathsf{p}+a}H_{\mathbf{C}}\cap H_{\zeta}\quad(\zeta=\exp(-2\pi\mathsf{i}a),\;a\in(-1,0]\cap\mathbf{Q},\;\mathsf{p}\in\mathbf{Z}-a),

\displaystyle W_{\ell}^{\widehat{\mu}}H_{\mathbf{Q},1}

\displaystyle=W_{\ell}H_{\mathbf{Q}}\cap H_{\mathbf{Q},1},\quad W^{\widehat{\mu}}_{\ell}H_{\mathbf{Q},\neq 1}=W_{\ell-1}H_{\mathbf{Q}}\cap H_{\mathbf{Q},\neq 1}

defines the desired quasi-inverse functor owing to the condition that (
2.1
) is a mixed Hodge structure.
The last statement follows from the similar one for
\MHS({\widehat{\mu}})
.
∎

The integers
\ell
such that
\gr_{\ell}^{W^{\widehat{\mu}}}H_{\mathbf{Q}}\neq 0
are called the
{\widehat{\mu}}
-weights of the object of
\MHS^{\widehat{\mu}}
.

Lemma 2.4

(Hodge symmetry and Hodge decomposition in
\MHS^{\widehat{\mu}}
)
.

Setting
(\gr_{\ell}^{W^{\widehat{\mu}}}H_{\mathbf{C}})^{\mathsf{p},\ell-\mathsf{p}}\!=\!\gr^{\mathsf{p}}_{F_{\widehat{\mu}}}\gr_{\ell}^{W^{\widehat{\mu}}}H_{\mathbf{C}}
(
\mathsf{p}\in\mathbf{Q}
), we have the Hodge decomposition

(2.4
\,*
)

\gr^{W^{\widehat{\mu}}}H_{\mathbf{C}}\simeq\mathop{\textstyle\bigoplus}\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{p},\mathsf{q}\in\mathbf{Q}\\
\mathsf{p}+\mathsf{q}\in\mathbf{Z}\end{subarray}}(\gr_{\mathsf{p}+\mathsf{q}}^{W^{\widehat{\mu}}}H_{\mathbf{C}})^{\mathsf{p},\mathsf{q}}.

Furthermore,
via the orthogonal decomposition
e_{1}+e_{\neq 1}
,
each object
((H_{\mathbf{Q}},T),F^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}_{\widehat{\mu}}H_{\mathbf{C}},W_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}^{\widehat{\mu}}H_{\mathbf{Q}})
in
\MHS^{\widehat{\mu}}
decomposes canonically as

((H_{\mathbf{Q}},T),F^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}_{\widehat{\mu}}H_{\mathbf{C}},W_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}^{\widehat{\mu}}H_{\mathbf{Q}})=((H_{\mathbf{Q}},T),F^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}_{\widehat{\mu}}H_{\mathbf{C}},W_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}^{\widehat{\mu}}H_{\mathbf{Q}})_{1}\oplus((H_{\mathbf{Q}},T),F^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}_{\widehat{\mu}}H_{\mathbf{C}},W_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}^{\widehat{\mu}}H_{\mathbf{Q}})_{\neq 1},

where the first term belongs to the category
\MHS
.

Proof.

Hodge symmetry on each
(\gr_{\ell}^{W}H_{\mathbf{Q}},F^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}\gr_{\ell}^{W}H_{\mathbf{C}})
translates to

\dim\gr^{\mathsf{p}}_{F_{\widehat{\mu}}}\gr_{\ell}^{W^{\widehat{\mu}}}H_{\zeta}=\dim\gr^{\ell-\mathsf{p}}_{F_{\widehat{\mu}}}\gr_{\ell}^{W^{\widehat{\mu}}}H_{1/\zeta},\quad\forall\zeta\in\mathbf{C}^{*},\;\mathsf{p}\in\mathbf{Q},\;\ell\in\mathbf{Z}.

The proof is then straightforward.
∎

Notation 2.5
.

We denote the corresponding decomposition of
\MHS^{\widehat{\mu}}
as

\MHS^{\widehat{\mu}}=\MHS\oplus\MHS^{\widehat{\mu}}_{\neq 1},

and the component of an object on
\MHS
is also called its
classical component
.

Remark 2.6
.

(1)

(The spectrum)
If
T
has order
m
, the Hodge polynomial

\sum_{\mathsf{p}\in\frac{1}{m}\mathbf{Z}}(\dim\gr^{\mathsf{p}}_{F_{\widehat{\mu}}}H_{\mathbf{C}})\cdot t^{\mathsf{p}}

was considered by Steenbrink
[
36
]
for the vanishing cycles of an isolated singularity of a holomorphic function and, in the case of a quasi-homogeneous isolated singularity, was compared to the Poincaré polynomial attached to the Newton filtration of the Jacobian quotient of the singularity.

(2)

(Relation with Anderson’s arithmetic Hodge structures)
Anderson has defined in
[
3
, §6.1]
the notion of arithmetic Hodge structure. We will omit his condition (6.1.5) for the moment, which is related to the rational structure of the de Rham component of the arithmetic Hodge structure. Any such structure is the direct sum of pure structures of some integral weight. Then the notion of pure arithmetic Hodge structure (without Condition (6.1.5) of loc. cit.) is related to that of a
{\widehat{\mu}}
-pure Hodge structure. Indeed, assume that
a finite-dimensional
\mathbf{Q}
-vector space
H_{\mathbf{Q}}

with the decomposition

H=H_{\mathbf{Q}}\otimes\mathbf{C}=\mathop{\textstyle\bigoplus}\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{p},\mathsf{q}\in\mathbf{Q}\\
\mathsf{p}+\mathsf{q}=w\end{subarray}}H^{\mathsf{p},\mathsf{q}}

is a pure arithmetic Hodge structure of weight
w\in\mathbf{Z}
. There exists a minimal finite set
A\subset(-1,0]
such that
H^{\mathsf{p},\mathsf{q}}\neq 0
implies
\mathsf{p}\in\mathop{\textstyle\bigcup}\displaylimits_{a\in A}(\mathbf{Z}-a)
. Let us set
A^{*}=A\cap(-1,0)
. Hodge symmetry implies
A^{*}=-1-A^{*}
. For
a\in A
and
\zeta=\exp(-2\pi\mathsf{i}a)
, one can define
H_{\zeta}
by summing the
H^{\mathsf{p},\mathsf{q}}
’s with
\mathsf{p}\in\mathbf{Z}-a
. Let
H_{\neq 1}=\mathop{\textstyle\bigoplus}\displaylimits_{\zeta\in\mu_{m}^{*}}H_{\zeta}
. Then, an arithmetic Hodge structure (without Condition (6.1.5)) is a pure
{\widehat{\mu}}
-Hodge structure if and only if the decomposition
H=H_{1}\oplus H_{\neq 1}
is defined over
\mathbf{Q}
: indeed, one defines the automorphism
T
as being equal to
\zeta\id
on
H_{\zeta}
, and the decomposition being defined over
\mathbf{Q}
is equivalent to
T
being defined over
\mathbf{Q}
.

We recall the notation for the tensor product of filtrations (indexed by
\mathbf{Q}
and decreasing, say)
(H^{\prime},G^{\prime})
and
(H^{\prime\prime},G^{\prime\prime})
,
see
[
37
]
:

(G^{\prime}\star G^{\prime\prime})^{n}(H^{\prime}\otimes H^{\prime\prime})=\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}=n}G^{\prime n_{1}}(H^{\prime})\otimes G^{\prime\prime n_{2}}(H^{\prime\prime}).

Definition 2.7
.

The tensor product

((H,T),F^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}_{\widehat{\mu}}H,W^{\widehat{\mu}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}H)=((H^{\prime},T^{\prime}),F^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}_{\widehat{\mu}}H^{\prime},W^{\widehat{\mu}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}H^{\prime})\otimes((H^{\prime\prime},T^{\prime\prime}),F^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}_{\widehat{\mu}}H^{\prime\prime},W^{\widehat{\mu}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}H^{\prime\prime})

is defined as usual by the formulas

\displaystyle(H,T)

\displaystyle=(H^{\prime}\otimes H^{\prime\prime},T^{\prime}\otimes T^{\prime\prime})\quad\text{(action of $\mathbf{Z}/(m^{\prime}m^{\prime\prime})\mathbf{Z}$)},

\displaystyle F^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}_{\widehat{\mu}}H

\displaystyle=(F_{\widehat{\mu}}H^{\prime}\star F_{\widehat{\mu}}H^{\prime\prime})^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}},

\displaystyle W^{\widehat{\mu}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}H

\displaystyle=(W^{\widehat{\mu}}H^{\prime}\star W^{\widehat{\mu}}H^{\prime\prime})_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}.

Proposition 2.8
.

The tensor product of two objects of
\MHS^{\widehat{\mu}}
is an object of
\MHS^{\widehat{\mu}}
, making
\MHS^{\widehat{\mu}}
, and hence
\MHS({\widehat{\mu}})
, an abelian tensor category.
The forgetful functor

\MHS^{\widehat{\mu}}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Vect^{\widehat{\mu}}
is a tensor functor.

Proof.

To check the first assertion, it is enough to check that
the two filtrations
F
and
W
on the tensor product deduced from
F_{\widehat{\mu}}
and
W^{\widehat{\mu}}
correspond to Scherk-Steenbrink’s definition of the join
[
34
, p. 661]
, and to use the results therein. We have

(2.9)

H_{\zeta}=\mathop{\textstyle\bigoplus}\displaylimits_{\zeta^{\prime}\zeta^{\prime\prime}=\zeta}H^{\prime}_{\zeta^{\prime}}\otimes H^{\prime\prime}_{\zeta^{\prime\prime}}\quad\text{and}\quad W^{\widehat{\mu}}_{k}H_{\zeta}=\mathop{\textstyle\bigoplus}\displaylimits_{\zeta^{\prime}\zeta^{\prime\prime}=\zeta}\sum_{i+j=k}(W^{\widehat{\mu}}_{i}H^{\prime}_{\zeta^{\prime}}\otimes W^{\widehat{\mu}}_{j}H^{\prime\prime}_{\zeta^{\prime\prime}}),

where the sum over
i,j
is taken in
H^{\prime}_{\zeta^{\prime}}\otimes H^{\prime\prime}_{\zeta^{\prime\prime}}
.

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

If
\zeta=1
, (
2.9
) reads

W_{k}H_{1}=\sum_{i+j=k}(W_{i}H^{\prime}_{1}\otimes W_{j}H^{\prime\prime}_{1})\oplus\mathop{\textstyle\bigoplus}\displaylimits_{\zeta^{\prime}\neq 1}\sum_{i+j=k-2}(W_{i}H^{\prime}_{\zeta^{\prime}}\otimes W_{j}H^{\prime\prime}_{1/\zeta^{\prime}}).

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

If
\zeta\neq 1
, (
2.9
) reads

W_{k-1}H_{\zeta}=\sum_{i+j=k-1}\bigl[(W_{i}H^{\prime}_{1}\otimes W_{j}H^{\prime\prime}_{\zeta})\oplus(W_{i}H^{\prime}_{\zeta}\otimes W_{j}H^{\prime\prime}_{1})\bigr]\oplus\mathop{\textstyle\bigoplus}\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\zeta^{\prime},\zeta^{\prime\prime}\neq 1\\
\zeta^{\prime}\zeta^{\prime\prime}=\zeta\end{subarray}}\sum_{i+j=k-2}W_{i}H^{\prime}_{\zeta^{\prime}}\otimes W_{j}H^{\prime\prime}_{\zeta^{\prime\prime}}.

Therefore, (
2.9
) reads

W_{k}H=\mathop{\textstyle\bigoplus}\displaylimits_{\zeta^{\prime},\zeta^{\prime\prime}}\sum_{i,j}W_{i}H^{\prime}_{\zeta^{\prime}}\otimes W_{j}H^{\prime\prime}_{\zeta^{\prime\prime}},

where the summation is taken over all
i,j
such that

i+j=\begin{cases}k&\text{if $\zeta^{\prime}=1$ or $\zeta^{\prime\prime}=1$,}\\
k-2&\text{if $\zeta^{\prime\prime}=1/\zeta^{\prime}\neq 1$,}\\
k-1&\text{if $\zeta^{\prime}\neq 1$, $\zeta^{\prime\prime}\neq 1$, and $\zeta^{\prime}\zeta^{\prime\prime}\neq 1$.}\end{cases}

This corresponds to the formula of loc. cit., up to an obvious typo there.
1
1

1

One should replace the condition
a=b=0
with
a
or
b=0
.
The proof for
F^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}
is similar: the formula of loc. cit. is

F^{p}H_{\zeta}=\mathop{\textstyle\bigoplus}\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\zeta^{\prime},\zeta^{\prime\prime}\\
\zeta^{\prime}\zeta^{\prime\prime}=\zeta\end{subarray}}\sum_{k,\ell}F^{k}H_{\zeta^{\prime}}\otimes F^{\ell}H_{\zeta^{\prime\prime}},

where the sum is taken over pairs
k,\ell
such that, setting
\zeta^{\prime}=\exp(-2\pi\mathsf{i}a^{\prime})
and
\zeta^{\prime\prime}=\exp(-2\pi\mathsf{i}a^{\prime\prime})
with
a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}\in(-1,0]
,

p=\begin{cases}k+\ell&\text{if }a^{\prime}+a^{\prime\prime}\in(-1,0],\\
k+\ell+1&\text{if }a^{\prime}+a^{\prime\prime}\in(-2,-1].\end{cases}

Let us set
a\in(-1,0]
satisfy

\begin{cases}a^{\prime}+a^{\prime\prime}&\text{if }a^{\prime}+a^{\prime\prime}\in(-1,0],\\
a^{\prime}+a^{\prime\prime}+1&\text{if }a^{\prime}+a^{\prime\prime}\in(-2,-1].\end{cases}

Then we have in any case
p-a=(k-a^{\prime})+(\ell-a^{\prime\prime})
, as wanted. One can also check that the decomposition (
2.4

∗

) behaves well by tensor product.
∎

3.
Perverse sheaves on the affine line

Notation 3.1
.

We will often use the following diagram involving the affine line (whose coordinate may take various names):

\{0\}\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\textstyle\kern 4.0pti\kern 3.0pt}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\longrightarrow}}}{}{}}\mathbb{A}^{\!1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\xleftarrow{\textstyle~j~}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j}}{{\longleftarrow}}}{}{}\joinrel\rhook}\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}.

3.a.
{\boldsymbol{k}}
-Perverse sheaves

We equip the affine line
\mathbb{A}^{\!1}
(coordinate
\theta
) with its analytic structure, which we denote by
\mathbb{A}^{\!1\mathrm{an}}
.
However, for the sake of simplicity,
we simply denote it by
\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{\theta}
or
\mathbb{A}^{\!1}
in this section.

We refer to
[
14
, Chap. 2]
for details on the next results. Let
\Vect({\boldsymbol{k}})
be the category of
{\boldsymbol{k}}
-vector spaces for some fixed field
{\boldsymbol{k}}
, and let
\Perv({\boldsymbol{k}})
be the category of perverse sheaves on
\mathbb{A}^{\!1}
of
{\boldsymbol{k}}
-vector spaces. The field
{\boldsymbol{k}}
being fixed, we simply write
\Vect,\Perv
. The additive convolution product
\star
on
\Perv
does not take values in
\Perv
. However, let
\Perv_{0}
be the full subcategory of
\Perv
whose objects consist of perverse sheaves with zero global cohomology. It is equipped with a tensor structure given by
\star
and there is a natural projector
\Pi:\Perv\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Perv_{0}
given by the additive convolution with
j_{!}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}}
. There is a functorial morphism
\mathcal{F}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Pi(\mathcal{F})
in
\Perv
whose kernel and cokernel are constant perverse sheaves.
For a perverse sheaf
\mathcal{F}
on
\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{\theta}
, we denote by
\raisebox{4.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{p}$}{\psi}_{\theta}\mathcal{F}
,
resp.

\raisebox{4.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{p}$}{\phi}_{\theta}\mathcal{F}
,
the space of nearby,
resp.
vanishing, cycles at
\theta=0
, equipped with its monodromy operator. The functor
\raisebox{4.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{p}$}{\phi}_{\theta}
decomposes with respect to eigenvalues of
the semi-simple part of the
monodromy as
\raisebox{4.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{p}$}{\phi}_{\theta,1}\oplus\raisebox{4.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{p}$}{\phi}_{\theta,\neq 1}

where the second subscripts indicate the range of the eigenvalues.
The two components behave differently when extended to mixed Hodge modules, and we have
\raisebox{4.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{p}$}{\phi}_{\theta,\neq 1}=\raisebox{4.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{p}$}{\psi}_{\theta,\neq 1}
.

The topological Fourier (or Laplace) transformations
\FT_{\pm}
, for which we refer to
[
17
, §VI.2]
and
[
25
, §1.b]
, transform objects of
\Perv_{0}
to perverse sheaves on another copy of
\mathbb{A}^{\!1}
, with coordinate
\tau
, say, of the form
\boldsymbol{R}j_{*}\mathcal{L}[1]
for some local system
\mathcal{L}
on
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}{}_{\tau}
. However, the datum of the perverse sheaf
\boldsymbol{R}j_{*}\mathcal{L}_{\pm}[1]=\FT_{\pm}(\mathcal{F})
is in general not enough to recover
\mathcal{F}
, as Stokes data for
\boldsymbol{R}j_{*}\mathcal{L}_{\pm}[1]
at infinity on
\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{\tau}
are missing in this description. The case of monodromic perverse sheaves is simpler, as shown by Lemma
3.2
below, where
\FT
is either the
+
or the
-
Fourier transformation, and
\FT^{-1}
is respectively the
-
or the
+
Fourier transformation. For
\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2}
in
\Perv_{0}
, we have
j^{-1}\FT(\mathcal{F}_{1}\star\mathcal{F}_{2})[-1]\simeq\mathcal{L}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{2}
. Taking the fiber at
1
of
(\FT\mathcal{F})[-1]
is a fiber functor for the Tannakian category
\Perv_{0}
.

3.b.
Monodromic perverse sheaves

Let
\Perv_{0}^{\mathrm{mon}}
be the full subcategory of
\Perv_{0}
consisting of perverse sheaves
\mathcal{F}
in
\Perv_{0}
whose shifted restriction
j^{-1}\mathcal{F}[-1]
to
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}
is a local system. It is a tensor subcategory of
\Perv_{0}
. The following is standard.

Lemma 3.2
.

(1)

The shifted restriction functor
j^{-1}({\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}})[-1]
induces an equivalence of categories

\Perv_{0}^{\mathrm{mon}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\text{local systems on $\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}$}),

with
j_{!}({\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}})[1]
as a quasi-inverse.

(2)

The vanishing cycle functor
\raisebox{4.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{p}$}{\phi}_{\theta}
at
\theta=0
induces an equivalence between
\Perv_{0}^{\mathrm{mon}}
and the category consisting of pairs
(V,T)
of a finite-dimensional vector space equipped with an automorphism. This equivalence respects the tensor structures on both categories.

(3)

The functor
j^{-1}\circ\FT({\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}})[-1]
induces an equivalence

\Perv_{0}^{\mathrm{mon}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\text{local systems on $\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}$}),

with quasi-inverse being given by
\FT^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{R}j_{*}({\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}})[1]
. By this equivalence, the additive convolution product corresponds to the tensor product of local systems.

Proof.

Let us only indicate the proof of (
2
). We can equivalently realize the category of pairs
(V,T)
as the category of local systems
\mathcal{L}
of finite-dimensional
{\boldsymbol{k}}
-vector spaces on
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}
. Then we have a diagram of equivalences

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 20.02783pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-20.02783pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Perv_{0}^{\mathrm{mon}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 113.13876pt\raise-28.55336pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\raisebox{4.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{p}$}{\phi}_{\theta}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern 233.49387pt\raise-8.00516pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise
