and thus isomorphisms for
?=\varnothing,{\mathrm{c}}
:

\gr_{k+1}^{W}\mathrm{H}^{k+1}_{?}(\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1},\widetilde{f}_{k})\simeq\gr_{k+1}^{W}\mathrm{H}^{k+1}_{?}(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}\times\mathbb{A}^{\!k},\widetilde{f}_{k}),

since the upper
resp.
lower rightmost non-trivial term in the diagram is pure of weight
k

resp.

k+2
. Furthermore,

\mathrm{H}^{k+1}_{\mathrm{mid}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1},\widetilde{f}_{k})\simeq\mathrm{H}^{k+1}_{\mathrm{mid}}(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}\times\mathbb{A}^{\!k},\widetilde{f}_{k}).

Let us also set

g(y)=\frac{1}{n+1}\,y^{n+1}-y,

and consider the convenient non-degenerate polynomial

(6.22)

g_{k}(y_{1},\dots,y_{k})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}g(y_{i}).

Under the change of variables
x_{i}=sy_{i}
on
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}\times\mathbb{A}^{\!k}
, and arguing as for (
5.15
) one has, for
?=\varnothing,{\mathrm{c}},{\mathrm{mid}}
,

(6.23)

\mathrm{H}^{k+1}_{?}(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}\times\mathbb{A}^{\!k},\widetilde{f}_{k})=\mathrm{H}^{k+1}_{?}(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}\times\mathbb{A}^{\!k},s^{n+1}g_{k}),

and a similar equality between the
\chi
-isotypical components with respect to the action of
\mathfrak{S}_{k}
permuting
y_{1},\dots,y_{k}
. We can thus apply Corollaries
5.14
and
5.22
to obtain the second point for
\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}}_{n}
.

Lastly, the pairing in the case of
\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}}_{n}
is obtained from the pairing

\mathrm{H}^{k+1}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1},\widetilde{f}_{k})\otimes\mathrm{H}^{k+1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1},-\widetilde{f}_{k})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{Q}(-k-1)

together with a change of variables similar to (
6.12
), and passing to the
\chi
-isotypical component.

In order to deduce the results for
\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai}_{n}
, one uses (
6.17
) for
?=\varnothing,{\mathrm{c}},{\mathrm{mid}}
.
∎

Corollary 6.24
.

We have the following identifications in
\EMHS^{\widehat{\mu}}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}},j^{+}\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}}_{n})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{H}^{k+1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1},s^{n+1}g_{k})^{\mathfrak{S}_{k},\chi},

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{mid}}^{1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}}_{n})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{mid}}^{k+1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1},s^{n+1}g_{k})^{\mathfrak{S}_{k},\chi}=W_{k+1}\mathrm{H}^{k+1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1},s^{n+1}g_{k})^{\mathfrak{S}_{k},\chi}.

Proof.

Notice that the localization sequences analogous to (
5.15
) (along
s=0
) yield, for
k\geqslant 2
, the canonical isomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{k+1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1},s^{n+1}g_{k})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{H}^{k+1}(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}\times\mathbb{A}^{\!k},s^{n+1}g_{k}),

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{c}}^{k+1}(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}\times\mathbb{A}^{\!k},s^{n+1}g_{k})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{c}}^{k+1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1},s^{n+1}g_{k}).

From Corollaries
5.14
and
5.22
we have canonical identifications

\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{mid}}^{k+1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1},s^{n+1}g_{k})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}W_{k+1}\mathrm{H}^{k+1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1},s^{n+1}g_{k}).

The desired identifications are obtained from the lower line of (
6.21
) together with (
6.15
), (
6.16
) and (
6.17
).
∎

In order to apply Proposition
5.17
we set, in a way similar to (
5.16
),

(6.25)

\widetilde{M}_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}=\image\bigl[({H}^{0}{}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}g_{k,!*}\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{p}$}{\mathbf{Q}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}_{\mathbb{A}^{\!k}})^{\mathfrak{S}_{k},\chi}\rightarrow\Pi_{\tau}(({H}^{0}{}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}g_{k*}\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{p}$}{\mathbf{Q}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}_{\mathbb{A}^{\!k}})^{\mathfrak{S}_{k},\chi})\bigr]\in\MHM(\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{\tau}),

which is pure of weight
k
, and we have
\Pi_{\tau}(\widetilde{M}_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}})=\Pi_{\tau}(({H}^{0}{}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}g_{k*}\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{p}$}{\mathbf{Q}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}_{\mathbb{A}^{\!k}})^{\mathfrak{S}_{k},\chi})
which has weights
\geqslant k
. Let us consider the exact sequence in
\MHM(\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{\tau})
:

(6.26)

0\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\widetilde{M}_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Pi_{\tau}(\widetilde{M}_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\widetilde{M}_{k}^{\prime{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0.

Lemma 6.27
.

We have
\widetilde{M}_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}=W_{k}\Pi_{\tau}(\widetilde{M}_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}})
and
\widetilde{M}_{k}^{\prime{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}}
is constant of weights
\geqslant k+1
.

Proof.

The cokernel of
\widetilde{M}_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}W_{k}\Pi_{\tau}(\widetilde{M}_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}})
is a constant pure Hodge module of weight
k
, which is thus a direct summand, hence contained in
\Pi_{\tau}(\widetilde{M}_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}})
. But the
\mathbf{C}[\tau]\langle\partial_{\tau}\rangle
-module underlying the latter mixed Hodge module does not contain any constant nonzero submodule, by definition of
\Pi_{\tau}
.
∎

Proposition 6.28
.

We have, for
p\in\mathbf{Z}
,
\zeta=\exp(-2\pi\mathsf{i}\varepsilon/(n+1))\neq 1
with
\varepsilon\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,

\displaystyle\dim\gr^{p}_{F}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{mid}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{s},\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}}_{n})_{\mathrm{cl}}

\displaystyle=\dim\gr^{p}_{F}[\rP_{0}\psi_{\tau,1}\widetilde{M}_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}(-1)],

\displaystyle\dim\gr^{p-\varepsilon/(n+1)}_{F_{\mathrm{irr}}}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{mid}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{s},\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}}_{n})_{\neq 1}

\displaystyle=\dim\gr^{p}_{F}\bigl[\rP_{0}\psi_{\tau,1}(K_{\zeta^{-1}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}\star_{\mathrm{mid}}\widetilde{M}_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}})(-1)\bigr].

Proof.

For the first equality, we identify
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{mid}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{s},\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}}_{n})_{\mathrm{cl}}
with
W_{k+1}\mathrm{H}^{k+1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1},tg_{k})^{\mathfrak{S}_{k},\chi}
, according to the second line of Corollary
6.24
and (

∗
∗
∗

‣
5.10
). Then we can argue as in the proof of
[
7
, Th. 3.2]
by applying
[
7
, Cor. A.31(1)]
to
\widetilde{M}_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}
.

The second equality follows from the second lines of Corollaries
5.32
and
6.24
.
∎

Contrary to the case of the symmetric power of the Kloosterman connection
\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Kl}}_{2}
considered in
[
7
]
,
j^{+}\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}}_{n}
is not the restriction to
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}
of the Fourier transform of a regular holonomic module like
\Pi_{\tau}(\widetilde{M}_{k})
because it is not of slope
1
at infinity (as can be seen from Lemma
6.7
). The Fourier transform of
\Pi_{\tau}(\widetilde{M}_{k})
will nevertheless prove useful in
7.e
when
n=2
. It also takes the form of a symmetric power
j_{+}\Sym^{k}G_{g}
, as asserted by the following lemma. In other words, the rational slope of
\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai}_{n}
at infinity leads to considering both expressions of
\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}}_{n}
:

[n]^{+}\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai}_{n}=\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}}_{n}\quad\text{and}\quad j^{+}\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}}_{n}\simeq[n+1]^{+}\Sym^{k}G_{g}.

Lemma 6.29
.

Let
G_{g}=j^{+}\FT\widetilde{M}_{1}
be the restriction to
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}
of the Fourier transform of
\widetilde{M}_{1}
and let
\FT\widetilde{M}_{k}
be the Fourier transform of
\widetilde{M}_{k}
. We have

\FT\Pi_{\tau}(\widetilde{M}_{k})=j_{+}j^{+}\FT\widetilde{M}_{k}\simeq j_{+}\Sym^{k}G_{g}.

Furthermore,
\FT\widetilde{M}_{k}\simeq j_{{\dagger}+}\Sym^{k}G_{g}
.

Proof.

We have
\Pi_{\tau}(\widetilde{M}_{k})=\Pi_{\tau}({H}^{0}g_{k+}{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{\!k}})^{\mathfrak{S}_{k},\chi}
, so

j^{+}\FT\widetilde{M}_{k}=j^{+}\FT\Pi_{\tau}(\widetilde{M}_{k})\simeq j^{+}\FT({H}^{0}g_{k+}{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{\!k}})^{\mathfrak{S}_{k},\chi}\simeq\Sym^{k}G_{g}.

For the second point, let us define
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
such that
\FT\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{k}=j_{{\dagger}+}\Sym^{k}G_{g}
. Since
\FT\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
is the minimal extension of
G_{g}
at the origin, it follows that
\FT\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{k}\subset\FT\widetilde{M}_{k}
(because
\FT\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{k}=\FT\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{k}\cap\FT\widetilde{M}_{k}
by minimality) and thus
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{k}\subset\widetilde{M}_{k}
. We wish to prove equality. Since
\FT\widetilde{M}_{k}/\FT\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
is supported at the origin of
\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{t}
, the quotient
\widetilde{M}_{k}/\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
is constant, and equality would follow from the property that
\widetilde{M}_{k}
does not have any nonzero constant quotient
\mathbf{C}[\tau]\langle\partial_{\tau}\rangle
-module. In order to prove this property, it is enough to show that such a quotient would underlie a quotient in the category
\MHM(\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{\tau})
. Indeed,
\widetilde{M}_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}
being pure, this quotient would be a direct summand, hence contained in
\widetilde{M}_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}
, and
\widetilde{M}_{k}
is known to have no nonzero constant holonomic submodule, being contained in
\Pi_{\tau}(\widetilde{M}_{k})
. Dually, it is enough to prove that the maximal constant
\mathbf{C}[\tau]\langle\partial_{\tau}\rangle
-submodule of a pure Hodge module on
\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{\tau}
underlies the maximal constant pure Hodge module. This follows from the theorem of the fixed part. In such a way, we have identified
\widetilde{M}_{k}
with
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
.
∎

7.
Proof of Theorem
1.1

We consider from now on the classical Airy equation
\partial_{z}^{2}-z
(the case
n=2
in Section
6
), and we set
\mathrm{Ai}=\mathrm{Ai}_{2}
and
f(x,z)=\frac{1}{3}x^{3}-zx
. The strategy of the proof of Theorem
1.1
is very similar to that developed in
[
7
]
for the Kloosterman case. It consists of

(1)

exhibiting a natural basis of
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
(Proposition
7.5
and Corollary
7.7
);

(2)

showing that this basis is adapted to the irregular Hodge filtration by lifting it to a suitable compactification of
\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1}
and by computing order of poles of representative differential forms along components of the divisor at infinity;

(3)

showing that this basis induces a basis of each graded piece of the irregular Hodge filtration by means of a duality argument.

Notation 7.1
.

We use the following notation and convention:

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

For integers
k\geqslant 1
and
m\in\mathbf{Z}
, we set

k^{\prime}:=\left\lfloor{(k-1)}/{2}\right\rfloor\text{ (i.e.,\ $k=2k^{\prime}+1$ or $k=2(k^{\prime}+1)$)},\quad m!\text{ and }m!!=1\text{ if $m\leqslant 0$}.

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

Since the cases where
k
is even and where
4\mid k

play a special role, we use the simplified common notation:

[1,k^{\prime}]\!]=\begin{cases}\{1,\dots,k^{\prime}+1\}&\text{if $k$ is odd},\\
\{1,\dots,k^{\prime}\}&\text{if $k$ is even},\end{cases}

7.a.
De Rham cohomology of
\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai}
and
\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}}

One checks that
\mathrm{Ai}
is the free
\mathbf{C}[z]
-module generated by the classes
v_{0},v_{1}
of
{\mathrm{d}}x,-x{\mathrm{d}}x
(see (

∗

‣
6.2
)). Moreover,
\partial_{z}v_{0}=-x{\mathrm{d}}x=v_{1}
and
\partial_{z}^{2}v_{0}=x^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x\equiv\penalty zv_{0}
, so that we recover the Airy equation
(\partial_{z}^{2}-z)v_{0}=0
.
Corollary
6.9
in this case reads

(7.2)

\displaystyle\dim\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},?}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}k^{\prime}+1&\text{if $k$ is odd},\\
k^{\prime}&\text{if $k$ is even},\end{cases}\quad\text{for $?=\varnothing,{\mathrm{c}}$},

\displaystyle\dim\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\dim\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})&\text{if $4\nmid k$},\\
\dim\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})-1&\text{if $4\mid k$}.\end{cases}

Let
[2]:s\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}s^{2}=z
be the ramified covering of
\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{z}
of order
2
given by
s\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}s^{2}=z
, and set
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}}=[2]^{+}\mathrm{Ai}
. Let
L=L_{-1}=({O}_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}},{\mathrm{d}}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}z}{z})
. Recall that
j:\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}}\mathbb{A}^{\!1}
denotes the inclusion.
We will set
L\otimes\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai}:=j_{+}(L\otimes j^{+}\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
.
Then

\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{i}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},L\otimes\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})=\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{i}(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}},L\otimes j^{+}\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai}),

which has dimension
3(k^{\prime}+1)
if
i=1

and is zero otherwise.
We have, on
\mathbb{A}^{\!1}
,

[2]_{+}\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}}\simeq\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai}\oplus(L\otimes\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai}),

and the decomposition

(7.3)

\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}})=\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})\oplus\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},L\otimes\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})

into
\mu_{2}
-character spaces.
More explicitly,
we have the isomorphism

[2]^{*}L=\Bigl({O}_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}},{\mathrm{d}}+\frac{{\mathrm{d}}s}{s}\Bigr)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}({O}_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}},{\mathrm{d}})

via multiplication by
s
.
Therefore, for an element of
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{1}(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}},L\otimes j^{+}\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})

represented by the global section

\eta\in\Gamma(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}},L\otimes j^{+}\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai}\otimes\Omega^{1})
,
we regard it as the class
s[2]^{*}\eta
belonging to the image of
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}})
in
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{1}(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}},j^{+}\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}})
.

We use the decomposition (
7.3
)
to obtain a basis.
The connection on
\mathrm{Ai}
is given by the matrix form

\partial_{z}(v_{0},v_{1})=(v_{0},v_{1})\cdot\begin{pmatrix}0&z\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}.

To treat the symmetric power moments and the twists together
in this subsection,
for
k\geqslant 1
and
\rho=0,1/2
,
we let
V_{\rho}
be the connection on
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}

V_{\rho}=\begin{cases}j^{+}\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai},&\rho=0,\\
L\otimes j^{+}\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai},&\rho=1/2.\end{cases}

Let
\Lambda_{\rho}=\Gamma(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}},V_{\rho})

be the differential module of global sections.
We fix the basis
{\boldsymbol{u}}:=(u_{a})_{0\leqslant a\leqslant k}
of
\Lambda_{\rho}

where
u_{a}=v_{0}^{k-a}v_{1}^{a}
,
in which the differential structure reads

(7.4)

z\partial_{z}u_{a}=\rho u_{a}+(k-a)zu_{a+1}+az^{2}u_{a-1},\quad 0\leqslant a\leqslant k,

with the convention
u_{-1}=u_{k+1}=0
.
Then the de Rham cohomology of
V_{\rho}

is given by the cohomology of the two term complex

z\partial_{z}:\Lambda_{\rho}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Lambda_{\rho}

\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{i}(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}},V_{\rho})=\mathbf{H}^{i}(\Lambda_{\rho}\xrightarrow{z\partial_{z}}\Lambda_{\rho}).

Proposition 7.5

(see
[
7
, Proof of Prop. 4.14]
)
.

(1)

Put
\Lambda_{\rho}^{+}=\mathop{\textstyle\bigoplus}\displaylimits_{a=0}^{k}\mathbf{C}[z]u_{a}
.
Then the
\mathbf{C}[z]
-module
\Lambda_{\rho}^{+}
is stable under
z\partial_{z}

and the inclusion of

(\Lambda_{\rho}^{+},z\partial_{z})
into
(\Lambda_{\rho},z\partial_{z})

is a quasi-isomorphism.

(2)

The cokernel
\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Lambda_{\rho}^{+},z\partial_{z})

has the basis

\displaystyle z^{k^{\prime}+1}u_{0},\cdots,zu_{0},u_{0},u_{1},\cdots,u_{k}

\displaystyle\quad\text{if $k$ is odd},

\displaystyle z^{k^{\prime}}u_{0},\cdots,zu_{0},u_{0},u_{1},\cdots,u_{k}

\displaystyle\quad\text{if $k$ is even}.

Remark 7.6
.

When analyzing the symmetric products
\Sym^{k}\Kl_{3}
of Kloosterman connections of rank three, Y. Qin
[
21
, §3.2.1]
has developed a slightly different method to obtain an analogous result.

Proof.

(
1
)
In fact, for any
r\geqslant 0
,
the lattice
z^{-r}\Lambda_{\rho}^{+}

is stable under
z\partial_{z}

and the induced map

z\partial_{z}:z^{-r-1}\Lambda_{\rho}^{+}/z^{-r}\Lambda_{\rho}^{+}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}z^{-r-1}\Lambda_{\rho}^{+}/z^{-r}\Lambda_{\rho}^{+}

coincides with the multiplication by
\rho-r-1

and is an isomorphism.

(
2
)
Define a degree map on
\Lambda_{\rho}^{+}
by setting

\deg z=\frac{2}{3},\quad\deg u_{a}=\frac{a}{3}.

Then
z\partial_{z}
is inhomogeneous of degree one.
On the associated graded module
\overline{\Lambda_{\rho}^{+}}
,
the induced map
\overline{z\partial_{z}}
is
\mathbf{C}[z]
-linear
with

\overline{z\partial_{z}}\overline{u}_{a}=(k-a)z\overline{u}_{a+1}+az^{2}\overline{u}_{a-1},\quad 0\leqslant a\leqslant k,

where
\overline{u}_{a}
denotes the image of
u_{a}
.

Assume
k
is even.
It is clear that
\ker\overline{z\partial_{z}}=\mathbf{C}[z]\overline{u}

where

\overline{u}=\sum_{i=0}^{k^{\prime}+1}(-1)^{i}\binom{k^{\prime}+1}{i}z^{k^{\prime}+1-i}\overline{u}_{2i}.

Inside the
\mathbf{C}[z]
-module
\mathbf{H}^{1}(\overline{\Lambda_{\rho}^{+}},\overline{z\partial_{z}})
,
the class
\overline{u}_{0}
is torsion-free,

\overline{u}_{2r+1}
are
z
-torsion for
0\leqslant r\leqslant k^{\prime}
,
and

z\overline{u}_{2r}\equiv\prod_{i=1}^{r}\frac{1-2i}{k+1-2i}\,z^{2r}\overline{u}_{0},\quad 1\leqslant r\leqslant k^{\prime}+1.

Furthermore one has (see
[
7
, Proof of Lem. 4.17]
for a similar argument)

\coker\Big[z\partial_{z}:\mathbf{H}^{0}(\overline{\Lambda_{\rho}^{+}},\overline{z\partial_{z}})\rightarrow\mathbf{H}^{1}(\overline{\Lambda_{\rho}^{+}},\overline{z\partial_{z}})\Big]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Lambda_{\rho}^{+},z\partial_{z})

and

\displaystyle z\partial_{z}(z^{r}\overline{u})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{k^{\prime}+1}(-1)^{i}\binom{k^{\prime}+1}{i}(r+k^{\prime}+1-i+\rho)z^{r+k^{\prime}+1-i}\overline{u}_{2i}

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{cases}c_{0}z^{k^{\prime}+1}\overline{u}_{0}+(-1)^{k^{\prime}+1}\rho\overline{u}_{k}&\text{if $r=0$},\\
c_{r}z^{r+k^{\prime}+1}\overline{u}_{0}&\text{if $r>0$},\end{cases}

for certain
c_{0},c_{r}\in\mathbf{C}
.
Since
\dim\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Lambda_{\rho}^{+},z\partial_{z})=k+k^{\prime}+1
,
one must have
c_{r}\neq 0
for all
r\geqslant 0

and the claim follows.

The case of
k
odd can be treated similarly and is easier.
Indeed in this case,

\det\overline{z\partial_{z}}=(-1)^{k^{\prime}+1}k!!z^{3k^{\prime}+3}

and hence

\mathrm{H}^{1}(\Lambda_{\rho}^{+},z\partial_{z})\cong\mathrm{H}^{1}(\overline{\Lambda_{\rho}^{+}},\overline{z\partial_{z}}
).
We omit the details.
∎

Corollary 7.7
.

(1)

The classes

\omega_{i}=z^{i-1}u_{0}{\mathrm{d}}z,\quad\eta_{j}=u_{j}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}z}{z}\qquad(i\in[1,k^{\prime}]\!],\,0\leqslant j\leqslant k)

(see Notation
7.1
) form a basis of
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{1}(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}},j^{+}\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
.

(2)

The classes

(7.7
\,*
)

\omega^{-}_{i}=z^{i}u_{0}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}z}{z},\quad\eta^{-}_{j}=u_{j}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}z}{z}\qquad(i\in[1,k^{\prime}]\!],\;0\leqslant j\leqslant k)

form a basis of
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{1}(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}},L\otimes j^{+}\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
.

(3)

The family
{B}_{k}=\{\omega_{i}\mid i\in[1,k^{\prime}]\!]\}
is a basis of
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
.

Proof.

Let
(\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})_{0}=\mathop{\textstyle\bigoplus}\displaylimits_{j=0}^{k}\mathbf{C}u_{j}

be the fiber of
\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai}
at
z=0
.
We have the exact sequence

0\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{1}(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}},j^{+}\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\textstyle\kern 4.0pt\Res\kern 3.0pt}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Res}}{{\longrightarrow}}}{}{}(\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})_{0}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0

where
\Res
denotes the residue map.
Statement (3) follows by noticing that

\Res(\omega_{i})=0

and

\Res(\eta_{j})=u_{j}
.
∎

Remark 7.8

(see
[
7
, Proof of Prop. 4.21]
)
.

Under the isomorphism (
6.11

∗

) for
?=\varnothing
, the classes
\omega_{i}
are mapped to

w_{i}=[z^{i-1}{\mathrm{d}}z\,{\mathrm{d}}x_{1}\cdots{\mathrm{d}}x_{k}].

7.b.
Middle de Rham cohomology of
\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai}

By (
7.2
),
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
is equal to
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
if
4\nmid k
and is of codimension one in it if
4\mid k
.
In order to compute a basis of
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
in the latter case, we define a family of numbers
\gamma_{k,i}
(
i\in\mathbf{Z}
) as follows. Consider the classical Airy functions
\Aif,\Bif
(see
[
1
, §10.4]
and
[
38
]
) which are entire functions on
\mathbf{C}_{z}
. Let
s=\frac{2}{3}z^{3/2}
.
As
z\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\infty
, we have the asymptotic expansions

(7.9)

\displaystyle\Aif(z)

\displaystyle\sim\frac{e^{-s}}{2\sqrt{\pi}z^{1/4}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}\frac{(n+\frac{1}{2})_{2k}}{54^{n}n!}\frac{1}{s^{n}},

\displaystyle|\arg z|<\pi,

\displaystyle\Bif(z)

\displaystyle\sim\frac{e^{s}}{\sqrt{\pi}z^{1/4}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(n+\frac{1}{2})_{2k}}{54^{n}n!}\frac{1}{s^{n}},

\displaystyle|\arg z|<\frac{1}{3}\pi.

Set
w=1/z
. The formal asymptotic expansion of
2\pi(\Aif\Bif)
at
z=\infty
is an element in
\sqrt{w}\,(1+w^{3}\mathbf{Q}[\![w^{3}]\!])
. It is the unique, up to scaling, formal power series solution in
\sqrt{w}

to the second symmetric power of the Airy equation
and one checks directly that it has a positive coefficient
in each degree
\sqrt{w}\,w^{3i}
,
i\geqslant 0
. We define
\gamma_{k,i}
by the formula

(2\pi\Aif\Bif)^{k/2}\sim\sum_{i\gg-\infty}\gamma_{k,i}w^{i}.

We then have

(7.10)

\gamma_{k,i}\begin{cases}=0&\text{ for $i\not\in k/4+3\mathbf{Z}_{\geqslant 0}$},\\
=1&\text{ for $i=k/4$},\\
>0&\text{ for $i\in k/4+3\mathbf{Z}_{\geqslant 0}$}.\end{cases}

Proposition 7.11

(see
[
9
, Cor. 3.11]
)
.

If
4\mid k
, the family

{B}_{k,{\mathrm{mid}}}=\{\omega_{i}-\gamma_{k,i}\omega_{k/4}\mid i\in[1,k^{\prime}]\!],\,i\neq k/4\}

is a basis of
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
, and
\omega_{k/4}
induces a basis of

\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})/\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai}).

Proof.

The second statement follows from the first one and Corollary
7.7
(
3
). For the first part, let

\iota_{\widehat{\infty}}:\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}(\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})_{\widehat{\infty}}

denote the formalization of
\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai}
at infinity, and
\widehat{\nabla}
the induced connection. We can represent elements of
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
by pairs
(\widehat{m},\eta)
as follows (see
[
8
, Cor. 3.5]
):

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

\widehat{m}
is a formal germ in
(\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})_{\widehat{\infty}}
, and

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

\eta
belongs to
\Gamma(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Omega^{1}_{\mathbb{A}^{\!1}}\otimes\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
,

such that, denoting by
\widehat{\eta}=\iota_{\widehat{\infty}}\eta
the formal germ of
\eta
in
\bigl[\Omega^{1}_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\widehat{\infty}}\otimes(\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})_{\widehat{\infty}}\bigr]
,
\widehat{m}
and
\eta
are related by
\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{m}=\widehat{\eta}
.

We can regard
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
as the image of the natural morphism

\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})

sending a pair
(\widehat{m},\eta)
to
\eta
. According to
[
8
, Rem. 3.6]
, there exists a basis of
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{c}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
consisting of

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

pairs
(\widehat{m}_{i},0)_{i}
where
(\widehat{m}_{i})_{i}
is a basis of
\ker\widehat{\nabla}

in
(\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})_{\widehat{\infty}}
,
and

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

a set of pairs
(\widehat{m}_{j},\eta_{j})_{j}
,
of cardinality
\dim\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
, related as above such that
(\eta_{j})_{j}
are linearly independent in
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
.

Furthermore, such a family
(\eta_{j})_{j}
is a basis of
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
. The proposition follows from Lemma
7.12
below.
∎

Lemma 7.12
.

Let us fix
i\geqslant 1
. If
4\mid k
, the subspace
\ker\widehat{\nabla}\subset(\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})_{\widehat{\infty}}
has dimension one and the equation
\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{m}_{i}=\iota_{\widehat{\infty}}(\omega_{i}-\gamma_{k,i}\omega_{k/4})
has a solution (in fact a dimension-one affine space of solutions).

Proof.

Assume
4\mid k
.
In this case,
the factor
L_{\mathsf{i}}^{\otimes k}
in the (unique) formal decomposition of
(\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})_{\widehat{\infty}}
in Example
6.8
is the trivial meromorphic connection
(\mathbf{C}(\!(w)\!),{\mathrm{d}})
, so
\ker\widehat{\nabla}
has dimension one.
For a
1
-form
\omega\in\Gamma(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai}\otimes\Omega^{1}_{\mathbb{A}^{\!1}})
,
we let

(\omega)_{\mathrm{reg}}\in\mathbf{C}(\!(w)\!){\mathrm{d}}w

be the regular part of
\iota_{\widehat{\infty}}(\omega)
in the decomposition of
(\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})_{\widehat{\infty}}
(twisted by
{\mathrm{d}}w
). Then
\iota_{\widehat{\infty}}(\omega)\in\image(\widehat{\nabla})
if and only if
\res_{w}(\omega)_{\mathrm{reg}}=0
. In order to compute
\res_{w}(\omega_{i})_{\mathrm{reg}}
, we consider the horizontal basis
\{e_{0},e_{1}\}
of
\mathrm{Ai}^{\nabla}

given by

e_{0}=\Aif(z)v_{1}-\Aif^{\prime}(z)v_{0},\quad e_{1}=\Bif(z)v_{1}-\Bif^{\prime}(z)v_{0}.

Then
v_{0}=\pi(\Bif e_{0}-\Aif e_{1})
. We note that, for any
i\geqslant 1
,

\displaystyle(\omega_{i})_{\mathrm{reg}}=(-w^{-i}u_{0}{\mathrm{d}}w/w)_{\mathrm{reg}}

\displaystyle=\pi^{k}\big([\Bif e_{0}-\Aif e_{1}]^{k}\big)_{\mathrm{reg}}\cdot(-w^{-i}{\mathrm{d}}w/w)

\displaystyle=\pi^{k}\binom{k}{k/2}(-\Aif\Bif)^{k/2}(e_{0}e_{1})^{k/2}\cdot(-w^{-i}{\mathrm{d}}w/w)

since
(\Aif\Bif)^{k/2}
is the only product among
\Aif^{i}\Bif^{k-i}

which has no exponential factor in its asymptotic expansion
by (
7.9
). Therefore

\res_{w}(\omega_{i})_{\mathrm{reg}}=-(-\pi/2)^{k/2}\binom{k}{k/2}\gamma_{k,i}(e_{0}e_{1})^{k/2}

and consequently, for
i\neq k/4
, there exists
\widehat{m}_{i}
satisfying

\widehat{\nabla}\widehat{m}_{i}=\iota_{\widehat{\infty}}(\omega_{i}-\gamma_{k,i}\omega_{k/4})
.
∎

7.c.
Proof of Theorem
1.1
for
k
odd

According to Theorem
6.18
, the de Rham cohomology
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
underlies a
{\widehat{\mu}}
-exponential mixed Hodge structure
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
of weights
\geqslant k+1
, which is pure of weight
k+1
and equal to
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{mid}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
if
4\nmid k
. In the case
k
is odd, we will first relate the irregular Hodge filtration
F_{\mathrm{irr}}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
of
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
with the basis
{B}_{k}
.

Lemma 7.13
.

If
k
is odd, we have

\omega_{i}\in F_{\mathrm{irr}}^{(k+1)-(k+2i)/3}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})\quad(1\leqslant i\leqslant k^{\prime}+1).

Proof.

From Corollary
6.24
we deduce an inclusion

(7.14)

\iota:\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})\mathrel{\lhook\joinrel\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}}\mathrm{H}^{k+1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1},s^{3}g_{k})

in
\EMHS^{\widehat{\mu}}
. This inclusion is strict for the irregular Hodge filtrations on each term, hence it is enough to check that
\iota(\omega_{i})\in F_{\mathrm{irr}}^{(k+1)-(k+2i)/3}\mathrm{H}^{k+1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1},s^{3}g_{k})
for
i
in the given range.

We begin with the compactification
\mathbb{P}^{k}
of
\mathbb{A}^{\!k}

with the boundary
\mathbb{A}^{\!k-1}_{\infty}
.
In fact,
\mathbb{P}^{k}
is the toric variety
associated with the Newton polytope of the Laurent polynomial
g_{k}
on
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}^{k}

and it is non-degenerate
(in the sense of Mochizuki, see
[
7
, §A.2]
)
for the pair
(\mathbb{A}^{\!k},g_{k})
.
Let
X
be the blowup of
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{s}\times\mathbb{P}^{k}

along
\{0\}\times\mathbb{A}^{\!k-1}_{\infty}

(the intersection of the divisors

\{0\}\times\mathbb{P}^{k}
where
s^{3}g_{k}
vanishes with order 3
and
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{\!k-1}_{\infty}

where
s^{3}g_{k}
has triple pole).
Let
P^{\prime}
be the proper transform of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{\!k-1}_{\infty}

in
X
.

Since
k
is odd, it is readily checked that
X
is a non-degenerate compactification
of
(\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1},s^{3}g_{k})
with boundary divisor
D=X\smallsetminus\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1}
.
On
X
,
the pole divisor
P
of
s^{3}g_{k}
equals

3(\{\infty\}\times\mathbb{P}^{k}+P^{\prime})
. The cohomology
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{k+1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1},s^{3}g_{k})
is computed as the hypercohomology on
X
of the twisted
P
-meromorphic de Rham complex

(\Omega^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}_{X}(\log D)(*P),{\mathrm{d}}+{\mathrm{d}}(s^{3}g_{k}))

and the irregular filtration
F^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}_{\mathrm{irr}}\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{k+1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1},s^{3}g_{k})
on the hypercohomology is induced by the filtration
F^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}_{\mathrm{Yu}}(\Omega^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}_{X}(\log D)(*P),{\mathrm{d}}+{\mathrm{d}}(s^{3}g_{k}))
by subcomplexes defined by (see
[
39
, (6)]
)

F^{\lambda}_{\mathrm{Yu}}\Omega^{p}_{X}(\log D)(*P)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }\lambda<0,\\
\Omega^{p}_{X}(\log D)(\left\lfloor(p-\lambda)P\right\rfloor)&\text{if }\lambda\geqslant 0.\end{cases}

In particular, the image of the natural map

\Gamma(X,\Omega^{k+1}(\log D)(\left\lfloor\mu P\right\rfloor))\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{k+1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1},s^{3}g_{k})

lies in
F^{k+1-\mu}_{\mathrm{irr}}\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{k+1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1},s^{3}g_{k})
. Let us consider the image by
\iota
of the basis
{B}_{k}
. We note that

z^{i-1}{\mathrm{d}}z\wedge{\mathrm{d}}x_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathrm{d}}x_{k}\in\Gamma(X,\Omega^{k+1}(\log D)(\left\lfloor\tfrac{1}{3}(k+2i)P\right\rfloor)).

It follows that
\iota(\omega_{i})
(
i=1,\dots,k^{\prime}+1
), which is equal to the image of
w_{i}
(see Remark
7.8
) in
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{k+1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1},s^{3}g_{k})
, belongs to

F_{\mathrm{irr}}^{(k+1)-(k+2i)/3}\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{k+1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1},s^{3}g_{k}).\qed

Remark 7.15
.

When
k
is odd, the functions
f_{k}
and
s^{3}g_{k}
are non-degenerate as Laurent polynomials. Therefore to conclude the above Lemma, one can also use the toric method of
[
2
,
39
]
via the inclusions (
6.11

∗

) and (
7.14
) respectively and restriction of the base space from
\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1}
to
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}^{k+1}
as adapted in
[
7
, §4.3.1]
. Instead of describing explicitly the Newton polytope of
f_{k}
or
s^{3}g_{k}
needed in the toric approach, here we compute the Hodge filtration by exhibiting a non-degenerate compactification which will appear again in the case of even
k
in Section
7.h
.

It will be convenient to define the decreasing filtration
G^{\mathsf{p}}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
(
\mathsf{p}\in\mathbf{Q}
) as

G^{(k+1)-(k+2i)/3}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})=\bigl\langle\omega_{j}\in{B}_{k}\mid j\leqslant i\bigr\rangle\quad(1\leqslant i\leqslant k^{\prime}+1),

and thus

(7.16)

\dim\gr^{\mathsf{p}}_{G}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $\mathsf{p}=(k+1)-(k+2i)/3$ for $1\leqslant i\leqslant k^{\prime}+1$},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

The statement of the lemma above amounts then to

(7.17)

G^{\mathsf{p}}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})\subset F^{\mathsf{p}}_{\mathrm{irr}}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})\quad\text{for all $\mathsf{p}$ and $k$ odd}.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
when
k
is odd.

Let us set
d_{\mathsf{p}}=\dim\gr^{\mathsf{p}}_{F_{\mathrm{irr}}}
and
\delta_{\mathsf{p}}=\dim\gr^{\mathsf{p}}_{G}
. Then, by the above inclusion,
\sum_{\mathsf{q}\geqslant\mathsf{p}}\delta_{\mathsf{q}}\leqslant\sum_{\mathsf{q}\geqslant\mathsf{p}}d_{\mathsf{q}}
for each
\mathsf{p}
with equality for
\mathsf{p}
small and for
\mathsf{p}
large. Furthermore, by Hodge symmetry (Remark
5.6
) we have
d_{k+1-\mathsf{q}}=d_{\mathsf{q}}
and, noticing that for
k
odd the symmetry
i\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}j=k^{\prime}+2-i
amounts to
k+1-(k+2i)/3\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}k+1-(k+2j)/3
,
we deduce from Formula (
7.16
) that
\delta_{k+1-\mathsf{q}}=\delta_{\mathsf{q}}
. As a consequence, we also have
\sum_{\mathsf{q}\leqslant\mathsf{p}}\delta_{\mathsf{q}}\leqslant\sum_{\mathsf{q}\leqslant\mathsf{p}}d_{\mathsf{q}}
for all
\mathsf{p}
, and it follows that
d_{\mathsf{p}}=\delta_{\mathsf{p}}
for all
\mathsf{p}
. Since
\delta_{\mathsf{p}}=1
for
\mathsf{p}
as described in the theorem, and zero otherwise, this concludes the proof.
∎

7.d.
Synopsis of the proof of Theorem
1.1
when
k
is even

For
k
even, the formulas in the theorem are equivalent to
\dim\gr_{F}^{\mathsf{p}}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})=1
for

(7.18)

\mathsf{p}=\begin{cases}\begin{cases}k/2-(2i-1)/3,\\
k/2+1+(2i-1)/3,\end{cases}&1\!\leqslant\!i\!<\!k/4\quad\text{if $4\mid(k+2)$},\\[12.0pt]
\begin{cases}k/2-2i/3,\\
k/2+1+2i/3,\end{cases}&1\leqslant i<k/4\quad\text{if $4\mid k$},\\
\quad k/2+1&\text{if $4\mid k$},\end{cases}

and
\dim\gr_{F}^{\mathsf{p}}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})=0
otherwise.

The argument used for
k
odd cannot be extended to this case since we are not able to prove in a similar way that the inclusion (
7.17
) holds for
every

\mathsf{p}
(see (
7.23
) below), and indeed
g_{k}
is a degenerate Laurent polynomial when
k
is even.
We will thus develop another method, similar to that used in
[
7
]
for the moments of the Kloosterman connection, relying on (ramified) Fourier transformation. This will need much more material, which we develop in Sections
7.e
–
7.g
.
Let us emphasize a new tool with respect to loc. cit., namely the inverse stationary phase formula with filtration, as expressed in
[
29
, (7)]
(see the proof of Proposition
7.29
). We explain below the main steps.

Step 1

In this step (realized in Sections
7.e
–
7.g
), we consider the pullback
\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}}
and the decomposition (
7.3
) and its mid analogue

\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{s},\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}})\simeq\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{z},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})\oplus\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{z},L\otimes\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai}).

We directly compute the irregular Hodge numbers
\dim\gr^{\mathsf{p}}_{F_{\mathrm{irr}}}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{s},\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}})
for all
\mathsf{p}
, relative to the irregular Hodge filtration considered in Section
6.c
.

For
\varepsilon\in\{0,1,2\}
, we consider the map

(7.19)

\displaystyle\rho_{\varepsilon}:\{j\mid 0\leqslant j\leqslant k,\;k+j+\varepsilon\not\equiv 0\bmod 3\}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{N}

\displaystyle j

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\left\lfloor{(k+j+\varepsilon)}/{3}\right\rfloor.

Note that, for
p\in\mathbf{N}
, we have
\#\rho_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(p)\in\{0,1,2\}
.

Proposition 7.20

(analogous to
[
7
, Prop. 4.20(2)]
)
.

If
k
is even, we have, for
\varepsilon=0,1,2
and
p\in\mathbf{Z}
,

\dim\gr^{p-\varepsilon/3}_{F_{\mathrm{irr}}}\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}^{1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{s},\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $\varepsilon=0$ and $p=k/2,k/2+1$},\\
1&\text{if $\varepsilon\neq 0$ and $p=k/2+1$},\\
\#\rho_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(p-1)&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

At this step, we use the techniques explained in
[
7
, App.]
together with Proposition
6.28
. Since the irregular Hodge filtration is compatible with the decomposition (
7.3
), it also remains to understand the action of
\mu_{2}
on the filtered vector space
\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}^{1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{s},\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}})
and, if
4\mid k
, to determine the irregular Hodge filtration on the dimension-one quotient space
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{1}/\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}^{1}
of
\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai}
. We will more precisely relate the irregular Hodge filtration with the filtration
G^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}
defined below by the basis constructed in Corollary
7.7
.

Step 2

We define the filtration
G^{\mathsf{p}}\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}})
as the filtration defined by the basis made of
\omega_{i},\omega_{i}^{-},\eta_{j}^{-}
for
i\in[1,k^{\prime}]
and
0\leqslant j\leqslant k
, such that

\gr_{G}^{k+1-(k+2i)/3}=\mathbf{C}\omega_{i},\quad\gr_{G}^{k+1-(k+2i+1)/3}=\mathbf{C}\omega_{i}^{-},\quad\gr_{G}^{k+1-(k+j+1)/3}=\mathbf{C}\eta_{j}^{-}.

Lemma 7.21
.

If
k
is even, we have, for
\mathsf{p}>k/2+1
,

G^{\mathsf{p}}\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{s},\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}})\subset\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}^{1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{s},\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}}),

and for
\varepsilon=0,1,2
and
k/2+1<p\in\mathbf{Z}
,

\dim\gr^{p-\varepsilon/3}_{G}\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{s},\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}})=\dim\gr^{p-\varepsilon/3}_{G}\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}^{1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{s},\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}})=\#\rho_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(p-1).

Proof.

The first point is clear if
4\nmid k
and follows, if
4\mid k
, from Proposition
7.11
and from the equivalence

k+1-(k+2i)/3>k/2+1\iff i<k/4.

For the second point, a direct check shows that

\dim\gr^{\mathsf{p}}_{G}\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}^{1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{s},\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}})=\begin{cases}2&\text{for }\mathsf{p}\in(k/2+1,2k/3+1/3]\cap\frac{1}{3}\mathbf{Z},\\
1&\text{for }\mathsf{p}=2k/3+2/3,\\
0&\text{for }\mathsf{p}>2k/3+2/3.\end{cases}

Assume for example
k\equiv 0\bmod 3
. Then one finds, for
p\in\mathbf{Z}
,

\displaystyle\rho_{0}^{-1}(p-1)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}2&\text{for }p\in[k/3+1,2k/3]\cap\mathbf{Z},\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\rho_{1}^{-1}(p-1)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}2&\text{for }p\in[k/3+1,2k/3]\cap\mathbf{Z},\\
1&\text{for }p=2k/3+1,\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\rho_{2}^{-1}(p-1)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}2&\text{for }p\in[k/3+2,2k/3+1]\cap\mathbf{Z},\\
1&\text{for }p=k/3+1,\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

The desired equality is easily checked in this case. The other cases
k\equiv 1,2\bmod 3
are checked similarly.
∎

When
k
is even, the analogue of Lemma
7.13
holds only partially.

Lemma 7.22
.

If
k
is even, we have

\displaystyle\omega_{i}

\displaystyle\in F_{\mathrm{irr}}^{(k+1)-(k+2i)/3}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})\quad\text{if }1\leqslant i\leqslant\left\lfloor k/4\right\rfloor,

\displaystyle\omega^{-}_{i}

\displaystyle\in F_{\mathrm{irr}}^{(k+1)-(k+2i+1)/3}\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{1}(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}},L\otimes j^{+}\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})\quad\text{if }1\leqslant i\leqslant k^{\prime}/2,

\displaystyle\eta^{-}_{j}

\displaystyle\in F_{\mathrm{irr}}^{(k+1)-(k+j+1)/3}\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{1}(\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}},L\otimes j^{+}\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})\quad\text{if }0\leqslant j\leqslant k^{\prime}.

The statement of this lemma, which is proved in Section
7.h
, amounts then to

(7.23)

\begin{aligned} G^{\mathsf{p}}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})&\subset F^{\mathsf{p}}_{\mathrm{irr}}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})\\
G^{\mathsf{p}}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}})&\subset F^{\mathsf{p}}_{\mathrm{irr}}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}})\end{aligned}\quad\text{for $\mathsf{p}\geqslant k/2+1$}.

Step 3: End of the proof

We conclude the proof of Theorem
1.1
in the case of even
k
as follows. Firstly, Proposition
7.20
and Lemmas
7.21
and
7.22
imply the equalities

F^{\mathsf{p}}_{\mathrm{irr}}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{s},\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}})=G^{\mathsf{p}}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{s},\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}})\quad(\mathsf{p}>k/2+1).

Restricting to
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
yields

F^{\mathsf{p}}_{\mathrm{irr}}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})=G^{\mathsf{p}}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})\quad(\mathsf{p}>k/2+1).

Since
\dim\gr_{G}^{\mathsf{p}}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})=1
for
\mathsf{p}\in(k/2+1,2k/3]
and is equal to zero for
\mathsf{p}>2k/3
, we find that the same property holds for
\gr_{F_{\mathrm{irr}}}^{\mathsf{p}}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
.

If
4\mid(k+2)
,
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})=\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{mid}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
is pure of weight
k+1
, and Hodge symmetry implies that
\dim\gr_{F_{\mathrm{irr}}}^{\mathsf{p}}\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})=1
for
\mathsf{p}\in[k/3+1,k/2)
. The theorem is proved in this case.

If
4\mid k
, Hodge symmetry yields the result for
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{mid}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
and it remains to obtain the last line in (
7.18
). In that case, the underlying vector space
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}
of the quotient

\mathrm{H}:=\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})/W_{k+1}\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})=\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})/\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{mid}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})

has dimension one with basis
\omega_{k/4}

(Proposition
7.11
).
Thus this
{\widehat{\mu}}
-mixed Hodge structure is a pure Hodge structure.
Since its weight is
\geqslant k+2
, the Hodge structure is of type
(p,p)
with
p\geqslant k/2+1
.
The first line of Lemma
7.22
shows that

\omega_{k/4}\in F^{k/2+1}\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}
,
hence the previous inequality is in fact an equality, yielding the last line of (
7.18
).

This completes the proof of Theorem
1.1
when
k
is even.∎

7.e.
The differential module
G_{g}
and its symmetric powers

We continue assuming
k
even, although this is not important in this section. We consider the function
g:\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{y}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{\tau}
,
g(y)=\frac{1}{3}\,y^{3}-y
and define
g_{k}(y_{1},\dots,y_{k})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}g(y_{i})
. The function
g
has two simple critical points
y=\pm 1
with critical values
\tau=\pm 2/3
,
upon which the vanishing cycle space is of dimension one and local monodromy equals
-\id
.

We take up the notation used in Lemma
6.29
. Let
\widetilde{M}_{1}={H}^{0}g_{+}{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{y}}
, that we regard as a
\mathbf{C}[\tau]\langle\partial_{\tau}\rangle
-module. The localized Fourier transform
G_{g}=j^{+}\FT\widetilde{M}_{1}
of
\widetilde{M}_{1}

on
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m},t}
is the cokernel of the
\mathbf{C}[t,t^{-1}]
-linear morphism

\mathbf{C}[t,t^{-1}][y]\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\textstyle\kern 4.0pt\partial_{y}+(y^{2}-1)t\kern 3.0pt}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{y}+(y^{2}-1)t}}{{\longrightarrow}}}{}{}\mathbf{C}[t,t^{-1}][y]

and is equipped with the connection
\nabla_{\partial_{t}}
induced by
\partial_{t}+g
. Then
G_{g}
is
\mathbf{C}[t,t^{-1}]
-free of rank two with basis
\{\widetilde{v}_{0},\widetilde{v}_{1}\}
, where
\widetilde{v}_{0}
is the class of
1
and
\widetilde{v}_{1}
that of
y
. The matrix of the connection in this basis is given by

\nabla_{\partial_{t}}(\widetilde{v}_{0},\widetilde{v}_{1})=(\widetilde{v}_{0},\widetilde{v}_{1})\cdot\biggl[-t^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}1/3&0\\
0&2/3\end{pmatrix}-\frac{2}{3}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\biggr].

One can check that it is irreducible (e.g. argue as in
[
35
, Ex. 8.19]
). It has a regular singularity at the origin with semi-simple monodromy having eigenvalues
\exp(\pm 2\pi\mathsf{i}/3)
. On the other hand, the formalized module
\widehat{G}_{g}
at infinity decomposes as
(E^{2t/3}\oplus E^{-2t/3})\otimes L_{-1}
, where
L_{-1}
is the rank-one
\mathbf{C}(\!(1/t)\!)
-vector space with connection having monodromy equal to
-\id
.

Since the determinant of
G_{g}
is the trivial rank-one bundle with connection, the differential Galois group of
G_{g}
is contained in
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbf{C})
. It is in fact equal to this group (argue as in loc. cit.). It follows that
\Sym^{k}G_{g}
is also irreducible and its monodromy at the origin is semi-simple with eigenvalues
1
,
\exp 2\pi\mathsf{i}/3
and
\exp-2\pi\mathsf{i}/3
. Indeed, the eigenvalues are the numbers (counted with multiplicities)
\exp(i+2j)2\pi\mathsf{i}/3
, for
i,j\in[0,k]
and
i+j=k
, that is, the numbers
\exp 2(k+j)\pi\mathsf{i}/3
with
j\in[0,k]
.

We will implicitly identify
\mathbf{Z}/3\mathbf{Z}
with
\{0,1,2\}
. For
\varepsilon\in\mathbf{Z}/3\mathbf{Z}
and
\zeta=\exp(-2\pi\mathsf{i}\varepsilon/3)
, we denote by
K_{\zeta,t}
the Kummer sheaf on
\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{t}
with monodromy
\zeta
around
t=0
(hence
\zeta^{-1}
around
t=\infty
) and we also consider similarly
K_{\zeta^{-1},\tau}
. We set
\widetilde{M}_{k,0}=\widetilde{M}_{k}
and
\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}=K_{\zeta^{-1},\tau}\star_{\mathrm{mid}}\widetilde{M}_{k}
for
\varepsilon\neq 0
. We note that, according to
[
6
, Prop. 1.18]
, we have

\FT\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}\simeq j_{{\dagger}+}(j^{+}K_{\zeta,t}\otimes\Sym^{k}G_{g}).

From the above computation of the monodromy of
G_{g}
we deduce, for
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Z}/3\mathbf{Z}
,

(7.24)

\dim\psi_{t,\exp 2\varepsilon^{\prime}\pi\mathsf{i}/3}(j^{+}K_{\zeta,t}\otimes\Sym^{k}G_{g})=\#\{j\in[0,k]\mid k+j\equiv\varepsilon^{\prime}-\varepsilon\bmod 3\}.

We note that, due to semi-simplicity of the monodromy at the origin, the minimal extension
j_{{\dagger}+}(j^{+}K_{\zeta,t}\otimes\Sym^{k}G_{g})
at the origin of
j^{+}K_{\zeta,t}\otimes\Sym^{k}G_{g}
satisfies

\displaystyle\phi_{t,1}\bigl[j_{{\dagger}+}(j^{+}K_{\zeta,t}\otimes\Sym^{k}G_{g})\bigr]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\phi_{t}\bigl[j_{{\dagger}+}(j^{+}K_{\zeta,t}\otimes\Sym^{k}G_{g})\bigr]

\displaystyle=\psi_{t,\neq 1}\bigl[j_{{\dagger}+}(j^{+}K_{\zeta,t}\otimes\Sym^{k}G_{g})\bigr]=\psi_{t,\neq 1}(j^{+}K_{\zeta,t}\otimes\Sym^{k}G_{g}).

At infinity, the formalized module satisfies

(j^{+}K_{\zeta,t}\otimes\Sym^{k}G_{g})^{\wedge}\simeq\mathop{\textstyle\bigoplus}\displaylimits_{j=0}^{k}\widehat{E}^{2(2j-k)t/3}\otimes(\widehat{L}_{-1}^{\otimes k}\otimes\widehat{K}_{\zeta^{-1},1/t}).

(Note that on the right-hand side, the monodromy of
\widehat{K}
is computed with center at infinity, while on the left-hand side it is computed with center at the origin, hence the change from
\zeta
to
\zeta^{-1}
.) This formal module has a nonzero regular part at
t=\infty
if and only if
k
is even, and in that case the regular component has rank one and is isomorphic to
\widehat{K}_{\zeta^{-1},1/t}
.

The monodromy at infinity (in the
\tau
coordinate) of
\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}
is semi-simple and is isomorphic to the monodromy of
\phi_{t}(j_{{\dagger}+}(j^{+}K_{\zeta,t}\otimes\Sym^{k}G_{g})
(according to the inverse stationary phase formula, which is a
{D}
-module variant of
[
23
, Cor. 5.20]
), and the latter space is nothing but the space
\psi_{t,\neq 1}(j^{+}K_{\zeta,t}\otimes\Sym^{k}G_{g})
. In particular,

(7.25)

\begin{split}\rk\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}&=\#\{j\in[0,k]\mid k+j\not\equiv-\varepsilon\bmod 3\}\\
&=\begin{cases}\begin{cases}2(\lfloor k/3\rfloor+1)&\text{if }k\not\equiv 0\bmod 3,\\
2\lfloor k/3\rfloor&\text{if }k\equiv 0\bmod 3,\end{cases}&\text{if }\varepsilon=0,\\[20.0pt]
\begin{cases}2\lfloor k/3\rfloor+1&\text{if }k\not\equiv 2\bmod 3,\\
2(\lfloor k/3\rfloor+1)&\text{if }k\equiv 2\bmod 3,\end{cases}&\text{if }\varepsilon\neq 0.\end{cases}\end{split}

On the other hand, since
\FT(\Pi_{\tau}(\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}))=j_{+}(j^{+}K_{\zeta,t}\otimes\Sym^{k}G_{g})
, we have

\displaystyle\rk\Pi_{\tau}(\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=\dim\psi_{1/\tau}\bigl[\Pi_{\tau}(\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon})\bigr]=\dim\phi_{t}\bigl[j_{+}(j^{+}K_{\zeta,t}\otimes\Sym^{k}G_{g})\bigr]

\displaystyle=\dim\psi_{t}\bigl[j_{+}(j^{+}K_{\zeta,t}\otimes\Sym^{k}G_{g})\bigr]=\rk\Sym^{k}G_{g}=k+1.

Let us set
\widetilde{M}_{k,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}=\mathop{\textstyle\bigoplus}\displaylimits_{\varepsilon\in\mathbf{Z}/3\mathbf{Z}}\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}
. We then find

\rk\widetilde{M}_{k,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}=2(k+1),\quad\rk\Pi_{\tau}(\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon})=3(k+1).

In the exact sequence

0\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}j_{{\dagger}+}(j^{+}K_{\zeta,t}\otimes\Sym^{k}G_{g})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}j_{+}(j^{+}K_{\zeta,t}\otimes\Sym^{k}G_{g})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}C_{\varepsilon}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0,

the cokernel
C_{\varepsilon}
is supported at the origin and is isomorphic to
\mathbf{C}[\partial_{t}]^{\nu_{\varepsilon}}
, where

\nu_{\varepsilon}=\dim\psi_{t,1}(j^{+}K_{\zeta,t}\otimes\Sym^{k}G_{g})=\dim\psi_{t,\exp{2\pi\mathsf{i}\varepsilon/3}}\Sym^{k}G_{g}.

By taking the inverse Fourier transform of the above exact sequence, we obtain the exact sequence

(7.26)

0\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Pi_{\tau}(\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\widetilde{M}^{\prime}_{k,\varepsilon}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0,

where
\widetilde{M}^{\prime}_{k,\varepsilon}
is constant of rank
\nu_{\varepsilon}=\#\{j\in[0,\dots,k\}\mid k+j\equiv\varepsilon\bmod 3\}
. As a consequence, there is an exact sequence

0\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\widetilde{M}_{k,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Pi_{\tau}(\widetilde{M}_{k,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\widetilde{M}^{\prime}_{k,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0,

where
\widetilde{M}^{\prime}_{k,{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}
is constant of rank
k+1=\sum_{\varepsilon\in\mathbf{Z}/3\mathbf{Z}}\nu_{\varepsilon}
.

The structure of
\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}
as a
\mathbf{C}[\tau]\langle\partial_{\tau}\rangle
-module is described by the following proposition.

Proposition 7.27
.

For
\varepsilon\in\mathbf{Z}/3\mathbf{Z}
the following properties hold.

(1)

The regular holonomic
\mathbf{C}[\tau]\langle\partial_{\tau}\rangle
-module
\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}
is irreducible of rank given by (
7.25
).

(2)

The monodromy of
\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}
at infinity is semi-simple with eigenvalues
\exp(\pm 2\pi\mathsf{i}/3)
.

(3)

The singular points
\tau_{j}
of
\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}
are the complex numbers
2(2j-k)/3
,
j=0,\dots,k
, and for each such
j
,
\phi_{\tau-\tau_{j}}\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}
has dimension one and monodromy
(-1)^{k}\exp(2\pi\mathsf{i}\varepsilon/3)\id
. In particular, since
k
is even,
0
is a singular point of
\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}
.

(4)

Furthermore,

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

if
\varepsilon\neq 0
,
\phi_{\tau,1}\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}=0
and
\psi_{\tau,1}\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}
has dimension equal to
\rk\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}-1
and monodromy equal to
\id
; on the other hand,
\psi_{\tau,\exp(2\pi\mathsf{i}\varepsilon/3)}\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}
has dimension one;

\scriptscriptstyle\bullet

if
\varepsilon=0
,
\phi_{\tau,1}\widetilde{M}_{k}
has dimension one,
\psi_{\tau,1}\widetilde{M}_{k}
has dimension equal to
\rk\widetilde{M}_{k}
and the nilpotent part
{\mathrm{N}}
of the monodromy on
\psi_{\tau,1}\widetilde{M}_{k}
satisfies
{\mathrm{N}}^{2}=0
.

Proof.

This follows from the stationary phase formula applied to
\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}
(see e.g.
[
24
]
) and the formal decomposition of
(j^{+}K_{\zeta,t}\otimes\Sym^{k}G_{g})^{\wedge}
at
t=\infty
computed above.
∎

7.f.
Weight properties of
\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}

Recall the exact sequence (
6.26
), with
\widetilde{M}_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}
pure of weight
k
.

Proposition 7.28

(analogous to
[
7
, Prop. 2.21]
)
.

For
\varepsilon\neq 0
, the
\mathbf{C}[\tau]\langle\partial_{\tau}\rangle
-module
\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}
underlies a pure Hodge module on
\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{\tau}
of weight
k+1
.

Proof.

Since
\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}=K_{\zeta^{-1},\tau}\star_{{\mathrm{mid}}}\widetilde{M}_{k}
for
\varepsilon\neq 0
, so that we can write
\widetilde{M}_{k,1}\oplus\widetilde{M}_{k,2}
as
\mathbb{E}_{m,\tau}\star_{{\mathrm{mid}}}\widetilde{M}_{k}
, we can enrich it with the structure of a pure Hodge module
\mathbb{E}_{m,\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}\star_{{\mathrm{mid}}}\widetilde{M}_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}
of weight
k+1
(the weights behave in an additive way under middle convolution). It follows that
\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}
(
\varepsilon\neq 0
) can be enriched with the structure of a pure complex Hodge module
\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}
of weight
k+1
. Interpreting
\Pi_{\tau}
as additive convolution with
j_{\dagger}{O}_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m},\tau}}
, and noting that
{}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}j_{!}(\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{p}$}{\mathbf{Q}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m},\tau}})\in\MHM(\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{\tau})
has weights
0,1
, we conclude that

\Pi_{\tau}(\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}):={}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}j_{!}(\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{p}$}{\mathbf{Q}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m},\tau}})\star\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}

has weights
\geqslant k+1
.
Furthermore, due to the irreducibility of
\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}
(Proposition
7.27
(
1
)),
we can argue as in Lemma
6.27

to conclude that
\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}=W_{k+1}\Pi_{\tau}(\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}})
.
∎

7.g.
Proof of Proposition
7.20

Recall the map
\rho_{\varepsilon}
defined by (
7.19
).

Proposition 7.29
.

The Hodge bundles
F^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}(\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon})
satisfy, for
\varepsilon\in\mathbf{Z}/3\mathbf{Z}
,

\rk\gr^{p}_{F}\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}=\#\rho_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(p)\quad\forall p.

Proof.

Since the monodromy of
\widetilde{M}_{k}
at infinity is semi-simple, the limit mixed Hodge structure of
\widetilde{M}_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}
at infinity is pure, and the graded pieces of the limit Hodge filtration have the same dimension as the corresponding graded pieces at a generic point of
\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{\tau}
. In order to compute
\rk\gr_{F}^{p}\widetilde{M}_{k}
, we are thus led to computing the ranks for this limit Hodge filtration.

For that purpose, we use the comparison with the limit at
t=0
of the irregular Hodge filtration of
\Sym^{k}G_{g}
, as proved in
[
28
]
3
3

3

We used this argument in the opposite direction in
[
29
, (7)]
.
in order to take advantage of the property that the irregular Hodge filtration on
\Sym^{k}G_{g}
is easily computed by means of the Thom-Sebastiani formula from that of
G_{g}
, hence is more directly accessible than the Hodge filtration of
\widetilde{M}_{k}
.

We first compute the jumps of the irregular Hodge filtration
F^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}_{\mathrm{irr}}G_{g,1}
of
G_{g}
at
t=1
. Since
{}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}g_{*}\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{p}$}{\mathbf{Q}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}_{\mathbb{A}^{\!1}}
is a pure Hodge module of weight
1
, it corresponds, away from the singularities, to a polarizable variation of pure Hodge structure of weight zero on
\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{\tau}
. The eigenvalues of the monodromy at
\tau=\infty
of the non constant part of
g_{+}{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{\!1}}
(the constant part has rank one and is given by the trace) being
\exp(\pm 2\pi\mathsf{i}/3)
, the irregular Hodge filtration
F^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}_{\mathrm{irr}}G_{g,1}
jumps at
1/3
and
2/3
(see
[
29
, (7)]
).

In order to compute the jumps of the irregular Hodge filtration of
j^{+}K_{\zeta,t}\otimes\Sym^{k}G_{g}
, we use that this filtration is obtained by tensor product from the irregular Hodge filtration of
K_{\zeta,t}
and that of
\Sym^{k}G_{g}
(Thom-Sebastiani).

On the one hand, by the same Thom-Sebastiani argument and the computation above, the jumps of
F_{\mathrm{irr}}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}\Sym^{k}G_{g,1}
occur exactly at
\lambda=(i+2j)/3
where
i,j
vary from
0
to
k
and
i+j=k
. We write
\lambda=k/3+j/3
with
j
varying from
0
to
k
.

On the other hand, if
\varepsilon\neq 0
, we regard
K_{\zeta^{-1},\tau}
on
\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{\tau}
as a complex Hodge module of rank one with Hodge filtration jumping at
p=0
only. Then, by
[
29
, (7)]
, the irregular Hodge filtration of its localized Fourier transform
K_{\zeta,t}
on
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m},t}
jumps at
\alpha
only, with
\alpha\in[0,1)
and
\alpha\equiv\varepsilon/3\bmod 1
. By using once more the good behaviour of
F_{\mathrm{irr}}
under tensor product, we find similarly that the jumps of
F_{\mathrm{irr}}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}(j^{+}K_{\zeta,t}\otimes\Sym^{k}G_{g})
occur exactly at
\lambda=k/3+j/3+\varepsilon/3
, where
j
varies from
0
to
k
and
\varepsilon/3\in(0,1)
.

Since
\rk\Sym^{k}G_{g}=k+1
, this implies that the jumps are all equal to one. For applying the inverse stationary phase formula, we do not care of the jumps at integers, and the one-dimensional jumps of
F_{\mathrm{irr}}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}(j^{+}K_{\zeta,t}\otimes\Sym^{k}G_{g})
(
\varepsilon\in\mathbf{Z}/3\mathbf{Z}
) at
p+1/3
,
resp.

p+2/3
for
p\in\mathbf{Z}
occur respectively for

(7.30)

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}(k+j+\varepsilon-1),\quad\text{for }j\in\{0,\dots,k\}\text{ such that }k+j+\varepsilon\equiv 1\bmod 3,

(7.31)

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}(k+j+\varepsilon-2),\quad\text{for }j\in\{0,\dots,k\}\text{ such that }k+j+\varepsilon\equiv 2\bmod 3.

Using
[
29
, (7)]
once more in the other direction, we find that the limit Hodge filtration on
\psi_{1/\tau}\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}
satisfies, for
\varepsilon\in\mathbf{Z}/3\mathbf{Z}
,

\displaystyle\dim\gr^{p}_{F}\psi_{1/\tau,\exp(2\pi\mathsf{i}/3)}\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1&\text{in Case \eqref{eq:p+1/3}},\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\dim\gr^{p}_{F}\psi_{1/\tau,\exp(-2\pi\mathsf{i}/3)}\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1&\text{in Case \eqref{eq:p+2/3}},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

We thus find the desired formula.
∎

Proposition 7.32

(Hodge filtration on
\psi_{\tau,1}\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}
)
.

Assume
k=2(k^{\prime}+1)
is even.

(1)

If
\varepsilon=0
,
\dim\gr^{p}_{F}\psi_{\tau,1}\widetilde{M}_{k}=\rk\gr^{p}_{F}\widetilde{M}_{k}=\#\rho_{0}^{-1}(p)
.

(2)

If
\varepsilon\neq 0
,
\dim\gr^{p}_{F}\psi_{\tau,1}\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}=\begin{cases}\#\rho_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(p)&\text{if $p\neq k/2$},\\
\#\rho_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(k/2)-1&\text{if $p=k/2$}.\end{cases}

Proof.

(1)

In this case,
\psi_{\tau}\widetilde{M}_{k}=\psi_{\tau,1}\widetilde{M}_{k}
(see Proposition
7.27
(
4
)), so the conclusion follows.

(2)

In this case,
\psi_{\tau,1}\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}
has codimension one in
\psi_{\tau}\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}

(Proposition
7.27
(
4
))
and we have to determine which value of
p
as given by Proposition
7.29
to put aside. We know that
\dim\phi_{\tau,1}\widetilde{M}_{k}=1
, and this space underlies a pure Hodge structure of weight
k
, hence of type
(k/2,k/2)
, so that
\gr^{p}_{F}\phi_{\tau,1}\widetilde{M}_{k}=0
for
p\neq k/2
. Recalling that
\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}={}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}j_{*}K^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}_{\zeta^{-1},\tau}\star_{\mathrm{mid}}\widetilde{M}_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}
, a proof similar to that of
[
6
, Th. 3.1.2(2)]
using
[
18
, Th. 1.2]
implies that

\dim\gr^{p}_{F}\psi_{\tau,\exp(2\pi\mathsf{i}\varepsilon/3)}\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}=\dim\gr^{p}_{F}\phi_{\tau,1}\widetilde{M}_{k},

and the conclusion follows.∎

Proof of Proposition
7.20
.

We continue assuming
k
even and we recall that we have set
k=2(k^{\prime}+1)
. We apply Proposition
6.28
.

(1)

If
\varepsilon=0
, the Lefschetz decomposition of
\gr^{W}\psi_{\tau,1}\widetilde{M}_{k}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}
reads
\rP_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}\oplus\rP_{0}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}\oplus{\mathrm{N}}_{\tau}(\rP_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}})
, and
\rP_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}
has weight
k/2=k^{\prime}+1
and dimension one. Therefore, the Hodge jump on
\rP_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}
is at
p=k^{\prime}+1
and that on
{\mathrm{N}}_{\tau}(\rP_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}})
is at
k^{\prime}
. The remaining jumps on
\rP_{0}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}}
are of size
1
if
p=k^{\prime},k^{\prime}+1
and of size
\#\rho^{-1}(p-1)
for the other values of
p
, according to Proposition
7.32
(
1
). We conclude with Proposition
6.28
:

\dim\gr^{p}_{F_{\mathrm{irr}}}\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}^{1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{s},\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }p=k/2,k/2+1,\\
\#\rho_{0}^{-1}(p-1)&\text{if }p\neq k/2,k/2+1.\end{cases}

(2)

If
\varepsilon\neq 0
, the monodromy of
\psi_{\tau,1}\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}
is equal to the identity, so that
\psi_{\tau,1}\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}=\rP_{0}\psi_{\tau,1}\widetilde{M}_{k,\varepsilon}
. Propositions
6.28
and
7.32
(
2
) give

\dim\gr^{p-\varepsilon/3}_{F_{\mathrm{irr}}}\mathrm{H}_{{\mathrm{dR}},{\mathrm{mid}}}^{1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1}_{s},\Sym^{k}\widetilde{\mathrm{Ai}})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $p=k/2+1$},\\
\#\rho_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(p-1)&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}\qed

7.h.
Proof of Step 2

We prove Lemma
7.22
(in a way similar to that of
[
7
, §4.3.3]
).

We start with
\omega_{i}
. We take up the construction and the notation of Lemma
7.13
. The singular locus
S\subset\mathbb{A}^{\!k}
of
(g_{k})\subset\mathbb{A}^{\!k}

consists of the
\binom{k}{k/2}
points

y_{i}=\varepsilon_{i}

with

\varepsilon_{i}=\pm 1

and

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\varepsilon_{i}=0

(where
g_{k}=0
has ordinary quadratic singularity).
Then
X
is non-degenerate away from
\{\infty\}\times S
.
Let
\varpi:\widetilde{X}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}X
be the 3-step blowup of
X
constructed as follows.
Let
X_{1}
be the blowup of
X
along
\{\infty\}\times S
.
Then on each component of the exceptional divisor
E_{1}
,
the rational function
s^{3}g_{k}

possesses an ordinary quadratic point.
Let
X_{2}
be the blowup of
X_{1}
along these points
with exceptional divisor
E_{2}
;
let
\widetilde{X}
be the blowup of
X_{2}
,
with exceptional divisor
\widetilde{E}_{3}
,
along the intersection of
E_{2}
and the proper transform of
E_{1}
.
Then by a direct checking,

\widetilde{X}
is a non-degenerate compactification of
(\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1},s^{3}g_{k})
.
In
\widetilde{X}
,
let
\widetilde{D}=\widetilde{X}\smallsetminus\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1}
be the boundary,

\widetilde{P}
the pole divisor of
s^{3}g_{k}

and
\widetilde{E}_{j}
the proper transform of
E_{j}

for
j=1,2
.
We moreover have

\ord_{\widetilde{E}_{1}}s^{3}g_{k}=-1,\quad\ord_{\widetilde{E}_{2}}s^{3}g_{k}=1,\quad\ord_{\widetilde{E}_{3}}s^{3}g_{k}=0.

Given a form

\omega=z^{r-1}x^{n}{\mathrm{d}}z\wedge{\mathrm{d}}x_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathrm{d}}x_{k},\quad r\geqslant 1,\ n=(n_{i})\in\{0,1\}^{k},\ \nu=\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i},

one checks that the pullback satisfies

(7.33)

\begin{split}\varpi^{*}\omega&\in\Gamma\big(\widetilde{X},\Omega^{k+1}(\log\widetilde{D})(\lfloor m_{r,\nu}\widetilde{P}\rfloor-(k-2r-\nu)\widetilde{E}_{2}-(k-4r-2\nu)\widetilde{E}_{3})\big),\\
m_{r,\nu}&=\frac{k+2r+\nu}{3},\end{split}

provided that
k\geqslant 4r+2\nu
(in order to have allowable pole order along
\widetilde{E}_{1}
).
In particular, if
\mu\geqslant\frac{1}{3}(k+2i)
and
i\leqslant k/4
(and since
k-2i\geqslant 2
),

\varpi^{*}(z^{i-1}{\mathrm{d}}z\wedge{\mathrm{d}}x_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathrm{d}}x_{k})\in\Gamma\big(\widetilde{X},\Omega^{k+1}(\log\widetilde{D})(\lfloor\mu\widetilde{P}\rfloor)\big),

and thus
\iota(\omega_{i})

defines an element in

F^{k+1-(k+2i)/3}\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{k+1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1},s^{3}g_{k})
.

Let us now consider the basis
\omega^{-}_{i},\eta^{-}_{j}
of
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},L\otimes\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})
. As in the proof of Lemma
7.13
,
let
\iota:\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!1},L\otimes\Sym^{k}\mathrm{Ai})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{H}^{k+1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1},s^{3}g_{k})

denote the inclusion in
\EMHS^{\widehat{\mu}}
.
In this setting, given a form

\omega=z^{r}x^{n}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}z}{z}\wedge{\mathrm{d}}x_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\mathrm{d}}x_{k},\quad r\geqslant 0,\ n=(n_{i})\in\{0,1\}^{k},\ \nu=\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i},

one has

\displaystyle s\varpi^{*}\omega

\displaystyle\in\Gamma\big(\widetilde{X},\Omega^{k+1}(\log\widetilde{D})(\lfloor m^{-}_{r,\nu}\widetilde{P}\rfloor-(k-2r-\nu-1)\widetilde{E}_{2}-(k-4r-2\nu-2)\widetilde{E}_{3})\big),

\displaystyle m^{-}_{r,\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{k+2r+\nu+1}{3},

provided that
k\geqslant 4r+2\nu+2
.
In particular,

[s\varpi^{*}\omega]\in F_{\mathrm{irr}}^{k+1-m^{-}_{r,\nu}}\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{k+1}(\mathbb{A}^{\!k+1},s^{3}g_{k})\quad\text{if $k\geqslant 4r+2\nu+2$}.

Now we have
\iota(\omega^{-}_{i})=\varpi^{*}\omega

with
r=i
,
\nu=0
,
while
\iota(\eta^{-}_{j})=\varpi^{*}\omega^{-}

where
\omega^{-}
is the average
(i.e., the symmetric projection of any)
of the forms
\omega

with
r=0
,
\nu=j
.
The claim follows.∎
